**Physical Description of the Volume**

This image shows the front cover and spine of a historical bound volume. The binding is a <term>half-binding|A bookbinding technique where the spine and corners are protected by leather or cloth, while the rest of the cover consists of paper or lighter material</term> style, featuring brown mottled leather on the spine and the corners of the boards. The boards themselves are covered in a patterned, <term>marbled paper|A decorative paper featuring swirling, stone-like patterns created by floating pigments on a liquid bath</term> with a feathered design in shades of tan, cream, and light brown. 

The spine contains five <term>raised bands|Horizontal ridges along the spine created by the structural cords used to sew the book's sections together</term>, creating six distinct compartments. Three of these compartments contain rectangular labels that are heavily darkened and worn, making any printed text almost entirely illegible. The paper covering on the bottom right corner of the front board is damaged, with a small piece missing that reveals a darker layer underneath.

# Aglais.

---

Aglais was young and inexperienced when he entered the world. Like the lonely flower in the quiet valley, Aglais grew up in a rural region, and, being unacquainted with people, he lived out the spring days of his innocence.

By the still brook, by the rushing spring, his soulful heart opened to the holy impressions of mankind’s teacher, Nature. The shining sun at midday, the purple glow of Aurora <note>The Roman goddess of the dawn; the author here personifies the sunrise as a divine teacher.</note>, the flower-filled meadow, and the singing lark were Aglais’s instructors.

From the blissful feeling of life, Aglais learned to know his God; and from the blessings of nature, Aglais learned the love of humanity.

<vocab>Aglais, Nature, Innocence, Love of humanity, Blissful feeling, Teacher, Instructors, Purple dawn</vocab>

<summary>This opening page introduces Aglais, a youth raised in pastoral isolation whose moral and spiritual character is shaped entirely by the natural world. It establishes the theme of nature as a divine instructor that instills both a love for God and a love for fellow human beings.</summary>

<keywords>Aglais, nature, innocence, education, Aurora, divinity, pastoral life, love of humanity</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the description of Aglais’s character, expanding on his virtuous, simple life in nature as a contrast to the "slavery" of modern human desires. It concludes with the catchword "Gegend" (region), which will complete the final sentence on the following page.</meta>

Far removed from all ignoble passion, the gentle youth knew no impressions other than those that are the noblest to mankind.

Aglais was content with what nature gave him; he slumbered peacefully under his cozy thatched roof, and his desires were as limited as his needs.

He owed his clothing to the benevolent sheep, his drink to the spring, and his nourishment to the grateful field; he was satisfied with what nature gave him, and his blood never boiled with the indignation of desire or greed.

He spared the kind herd that clothed him and never shed the blood of the faithful ox <note>This implies a deep moral respect for animals, suggesting that Aglais lived in such harmony with nature that he refused to kill the creatures that assisted him.</note> who laboriously plowed his field and nourished him.

Your happiness, O people, lies in nature; do not accuse creation of cruelty just because you drag the chains of slavery <note>In this context, "slavery" refers to the self-imposed shackles of civilization, such as greed, social status, and artificial needs, rather than literal servitude.</note> far behind you.

The deity created you for bliss and delight, but you could not bear your happiness; you yourselves have turned paradise into a desert. This excellent Eden still stands; but, overgrown with thorns and thistles, few can still find the entrance into this heavenly

<vocab>Aglais, Nature, Paradise, Eden, Slavery, Passion, Creation</vocab>

<summary>This page depicts Aglais as a model of contentment and non-violence, living a simple life sustained by nature without greed. It serves as a philosophical critique of humanity, arguing that people have abandoned a divinely intended "Eden" for a self-made "desert" of artificial desires.</summary>
<keywords>Aglais, pastoral virtue, simplicity, non-violence, Eden, critique of society, contentment, nature vs. civilization</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 4; the catchword "Gefühl" (Feeling) from the previous page leads into the description of the landscape and the inner state of the protagonist.</meta>

region; only for the one who knows the true treasures of life is the path still cleared—and these treasures are wisdom and virtue.

Despised and mocked is he who walks this path; lonely as a pilgrim must he wander who wishes to reach this place; for men regard it as an insult when someone has the courage to throw away his fool's cap <note>The "Narrenkappe" or fool's cap symbolizes social conformity to vanity and superficiality.</note>. It has become the fashion, and refinement knew how to give the fool's cap a thousand shapes and cuts; the bells are sewn on so artfully that one must have a fine ear to hear them jingle.

Aglais did not know the world, and was happy. His guiltless heart knew no joy but that of innocence.

Just as the rosebud opens to the breath of the lovely Zephyr <note>The West Wind in Greek mythology, associated with spring.</note>, so Aglais's heart opened toward everyone, and did not yet know that falsehood and malice disfigured mankind.

The rose had already bloomed fifteen times at Aglais's cottage, and fifteen times the grape became ripe, since Aglais first saw the world <note>A poetic way of stating Aglais is fifteen years old.</note>.

Aglais was a youth of noble courage and a free soul; his way of thinking was unfeigned, and his heart was open. His nerves were for

## Poor August.

---

->Great, Mighty, and Rich! Read my true,<-
->terrible story! Let it be laid upon your heart,<-
and when you are invited to a magnificent feast
and pass by a poor man, share with him even
the smallest piece from your full gaming-purse.
He, being sated, will bless you in the evening;
but he will curse you if you answer his urgent
pleading with a shake of the head, and you shall
one day find this curse recorded in the debt-ledger
of the Eternal <note>A reference to God as the "Eternal Judge" who keeps a record of human deeds.</note>, who
gave you abundance so that you should clothe the
naked and feed the hungry.

In M — in S — <note>Likely abbreviations for specific towns or regions, common in 18th-century literature to provide a sense of realism while protecting identities.</note> there lived, about sixty
years ago, a poor linen weaver. Scantily he
nourished

<meta>The previous page likely introduced the humble circumstances of the weaver's family; the catchword "zeich," at the end of this page anticipates the word "Verzeichniß" (list/register) on the following page.</meta>

his trade nourished him, and often his table was set only once a day; but he lived happy and content, for God had blessed his marriage with a promising son, who from his infancy had been the joy of his poor parents. His godfather, the pastor of the small town, allowed August—for that was the boy's name—to study, and when the old <term>linen weaver<note>German: Leinweber. A common but often low-paying craft in the 18th and 19th centuries.</note></term> crept to the parsonage on Sundays, the pastor often read him letters from the professors, who could not praise his pupil enough. With tears of joy in his eyes, the happy father returned to his hut and, during their nightly meal, told his wife of their dear son’s praise; then, potatoes tasted more delicious to the old couple than the finest roast meat.

August had studied <term>the law<note>German: die Rechte. Referring to a university degree in jurisprudence.</note></term> and returned, provided with the best testimonials, into the arms of his parents and the house of his benefactor. Shortly thereafter the pastor died, and August had to use every effort to protect himself and his poor parents from want. The <term>chief tax collector<note>German: Obereinnehmer. A local administrative official responsible for revenue.</note></term> of the small town was promoted; August dared to apply for this vacant position.

The Minister of this <term>department<note>German: Departements. A high-ranking government administrative division.</note></term>, a worthy and upright man, had the professors of the national university send him every year a regis—

# Page 9

...send in a list of the most diligent students, and since August stood at the very top of this list, he received the preference over thirty fellow competitors. Within four weeks he was a <term>Chief Tax Collector</term> <note>German: Obereinnehmer; a high-ranking financial official in the civil service</note>, and had an annual salary of five hundred <term>Thalers</term> <note>A silver coin used throughout Europe for centuries; this was a very comfortable middle-class income at the time</note>, consequently more than his highest wish had ever desired. His poor father wove linen no longer; he lived with his wife in the house of their grateful son. Both enjoyed a most peaceful old age for several more years, and finally died in the arms of their August. Their last word, their last look, was a blessing upon him. The old folks had already been resting in their graves for a whole year when August was once invited to a ball in a neighboring small town. He came, saw there the beautiful Amalia, and on the way back felt for the first time the urge of love.

There exists a certain class of people who, guided by erroneous concepts, make a crime out of the most innocent love, seek to hide it from all eyes, and imagine they are committing the greatest vice if they should reveal this passion to their best friend. August belonged to this class; he locked his love in the innermost depths of his heart, tormented himself for half a year, and since he could no longer endure his agony, he traveled in the greatest silence

<summary>August achieves financial success as a Chief Tax Collector, cares for his parents until their death, and eventually falls in secret, agonizing love with a woman named Amalia.</summary>

--- Page 16 ---
to the girl's place of residence, proposed in writing for Amalie's hand, and returned married before a single inhabitant of his hometown had even suspected his love. His marriage was now the talk of the entire little town. Everyone pitied the good August, for he had taken a girl who stood in poor repute throughout the whole region. Everyone knew—except for the secretive August—that a few years prior she had been the public mistress <note>original: "Maitresse"</note> of a nobleman, had conceived a child with him, and because of her bad conduct had been ignominiously chased from the castle. No one was so cruel as to tell him this story, and so August lived peacefully and contentedly with his wife, who, after the first year, bore him a daughter, her perfect image. Already many were taking back their first judgment regarding this marriage, because they hoped with good reason that Amalie—warned by her first fall—would now be and remain a good wife. But Amalie's change was not lasting; she began to host small dinner parties <note>original: "Tafeln"</note> and small games, inviting all sorts of people, and among them all the handsome officers of the garrison. Often August stayed awake the entire night on his lonely bed, until his wife, only in the late morning, shared it with him. He felt—

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>The catchword "Er" (He) from the previous page continues the narrative of August's internal struggle with his wife's spending habits.</meta>

...that this was not right; but he was too in love, and consequently too weak, to put a stop to the whole affair. Finally, he saw that his entire salary was not sufficient to cover his wife's expenses, and creditors <note>original: "Schuldner"</note> began to appear, whom he had to put off until the following year. He grumbled at her for the first time, but a fiery embrace and a sweet kiss stilled his anger, and his house soon became the gathering place for all sorts of young and debauched people. A better position in a larger city was now open. August, who until now had always fulfilled his duty with the greatest precision and had frequently received decrees of commendation <note>official certificates of praise for civil service</note> for it, hoped—not without reason—that he would obtain this post. He believed he could put a complete stop to his wife's excesses if he removed her from such dangerous company. He revealed his initial intention to Amalia; she not only approved of it, but also advised him to travel to the residence city <note>the capital city where the Prince or Sovereign resides</note> himself, in order to better pursue his request. August took the smallest portion of the little cash they had for the journey, leaving his wife even more than she needed for a quiet, domestic household <note>original: "Wirthschaft"</note>. As he was already sitting in the carriage, he gave her the key to the sovereign's treasury <note>the official state funds or tax chest he was responsible for as an official</note>, so that she could save everything in the event that a fire should break out.

<summary>August attempts to solve his financial troubles by seeking a promotion in the capital, but he makes the fatal mistake of leaving his wife in charge of the state treasury keys.</summary>

He arrived at the <term>residence</term><note>The city where the sovereign or high officials reside, often the administrative capital.</note>; the minister was away on a journey, and he was forced to wait fourteen days for his arrival. In the meantime, he wrote the most touching letters to his wife, presenting to her in writing—which he was better able to do than in person—the consequences that her extravagance would have, and necessarily must have. August expected much from the impression these letters would make on her, but Amalie threw them aside unread, carousing in the most exaggerated manner with her admirers; attentive observers calculated with the greatest precision that a single <term>soupee</term><note>A late-night supper party, often lavish and expensive.</note> often cost sixty or more <term>thalers</term><note>A large silver coin used throughout Europe for centuries; sixty thalers was a significant sum, roughly equivalent to several months' wages for a skilled laborer.</note>.

Everyone has their enemies, August included, and before the minister returned to the residence, orders had already been given—according to reports received—to <term>audit</term><note>original: "revidiren"</note> August’s official accounts.

Early in the morning, he presented his request to the newly arrived minister.

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>The catchword "Father" from the previous page suggests a transition into the family dynamics or the paternal legacy of the protagonist, August.</meta>

as soon as the cash box had been sealed, she traveled hastily into the country. August knew that he was to be audited, so the sealing did not come to him unexpectedly; but his wife's journey, and her long absence, did not let him sleep the entire night. In the early morning, deputies arrived, opened the cash box in August's presence, and instead of nine hundred <term>Thalers</term> <note>A large silver coin used throughout Europe for centuries; 900 Thalers would have been a substantial sum, likely several years' wages for a clerk.</note>, there were only fifty lying within. The deficit exceeded his <term>security bond</term> <note>original: "Caution"; a financial guarantee paid upon taking a public office to cover potential losses.</note>, which he had provided through the legacy of his godfather, by half, and the astonished August was led to prison. He still did not know what he should believe or what he should think, when his best friend entered the prison to see him and told him of his wife's entire conduct during his absence. "I gave the key to that frivolous woman," was all he said, and in all the interrogations he declared himself the guilty party, regardless of the fact that his judges—well-acquainted with the whole matter—often asked him: whether his wife had not perhaps made raids upon the cash box? According to his own confession, he was sentenced—still very leniently according to the laws of the land—to five years of labor on the <term>fortress-works</term> <note>original: "Festungsbaue"; a common form of penal labor involving the heavy construction or repair of military fortifications.</note>.

The innocent man wheeled his cart for this long time without grumbling, and hurried, when he was released, to his

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>The catchword "Amalie" from the previous page likely refers to the wife's name or a related character, though she is referred to here as the "housekeeper" or "Miss." This page continues the narrative of August's release and his search for his family.</meta>

toward his native city. He asked eagerly after his wife and after his child. The latter had been nourished by merciful people until now, and they willingly gave the child back to the pleading father. As for the former, he learned after long investigation that she was staying in P**** <note>Likely a redacted place name, common in 18th-century literature to protect identities or add realism</note> as a housekeeper for a Colonel. With his child on his arm, he traveled to her. Perhaps the unfortunate man believed that his wife, because he had suffered the penalty for her sake, would receive him with open arms and give him all her love once again. When he arrived there, he asked for the "Mrs. Housekeeper," and heard to his greatest astonishment that they would call "the Miss" <note>original: "Mamsell" – a title for an unmarried woman of some status, indicating she was passing herself off as single</note> immediately. She came, and the ungrateful woman would not even recognize him. "I was never married," she said boldly! "The fellow must be a fool!" and August was beaten out of the house by a corporal! No prospect, no help, not even a single comfort! He begged his way with his child to the next town. There he was so fortunate as to obtain a small room for rent and, because of his good handwriting, received legal documents for copying from many lawyers <term>Advokaten: attorneys or legal advocates</term>. Scantily, but honestly, he nourished himself with this work for eight long years. His Lottchen, his daughter, was the only comfort, the only pleasure that he had and knew! She loved him above all else; his eye had only to beckon, and his sign was followed.

<summary>After being released from prison, August finds his daughter but is cruelly rejected and physically cast out by his wife, who denies their marriage. He spends the next eight years living in poverty, working as a legal scribe and raising his devoted daughter, Lottchen.</summary>
<keywords>betrayal, fatherhood, poverty, legal scribe, social status, domestic tragedy</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>Continuity from page 14: The narrative follows the diverging paths of Amalie and August. Catchword "sich" (themselves) connects to the beginning of the next page.</meta>

Amalie had meanwhile already left her Colonel and had come to the city. A young Minister still found her charming, and her fate had become very brilliant through his generosity. Once, August was carrying transcribed files home when he encountered a magnificent carriage in the main street; he tried to step aside, saw his wife sitting inside, stood still in astonishment, and was driven to the ground by the merciless coachman. The carriage rolled rapidly away, and August lay there as if dead. His right arm was shattered, his left leg severely damaged. Kind-hearted people carried him home once he was able to speak; his desolate daughter called for a <term>Surgeon<note>original: "Chyrurgus"; an early medical practitioner who performed manual operations and treated wounds, often distinct from university-trained physicians.</note></term>, and this man only restored him after half a year, paralyzed in hand and foot. When the surgeon demanded his payment, father and daughter had only a single dress left; their few household goods had been sold except for one table and one chair. All of this and their only bed was hardly enough to satisfy his demand. In the evening they went to sleep hungry for the first time, and in the morning they did not know where they should get a morsel of bread for that day. It was already noon, and still no fire burned on their hearth. Both wept, but both remained silent, because they

The father was the first to break the silence:

**August:** Lottchen <note>A diminutive form of Charlotte</note>, what will become of us?

**Lottchen:** God in heaven knows!

**August:** My hand is paralyzed; I can no longer write! And even if I wanted to go out, if I wanted to ask kind people for alms <term>alms: charitable donations of money or food given to the poor</term>, I do not even have a coat. If God does not take pity on us soon, we must die of hunger!

**Lottchen:** Father, I will — — — (Tears prevent her from speaking further.)

**August:** What will you do?

**Lottchen:** I — will — go — begging!

**August:** You? You? And yet — but no! I know of another, less terrible means! Your mother is in the city; it was her carriage that knocked me to the ground.

**Lottchen:** Oh Jesus!

**August:** It came from the Minister’s house; she must live there, or at least be able to be found there. You are her daughter; you lay beneath her heart, her blood nourished you; she will [show] mer- <note>The sentence breaks off here, continuing on the next page with "mercy" (Erbarmung/Barmherzigkeit).</note>

<meta>Continuity from page 16: The narrative continues the struggle of August and his daughter Lottchen in their state of extreme poverty and desperation.</meta>

have mercy on you, and if you are provided for, then I will gladly die. Inquire after her using my—using her—maiden name. Describe our misery to her, and if this does not move her, then tell her boldly that we are unhappy because of her.

**Lottchen.** (joyfully) I am to see my mother! Oh, how fortunate for us that she still lives! She will certainly help us! I am going at once!

**August.** Go, child, go. I am very hungry! In the meantime, I will pray that God may soften her heart!

Lottchen hurried away, and August knelt down, begging God for a hearing, for mercy. Under August’s name, no one in the Minister’s <note>A high-ranking government official or statesman</note> house wanted to know anything of Amalie; but when she mentioned her maiden name, everyone knew that it was the gracious young lady. But she was already sitting at the table <note>original: "Tafel" - referring to a formal, multi-course dinner</note>, and her daughter had to wait, hungry, in the anteroom <note>original: "Antichambre" - a waiting room for guests in a grand house</note> until five o'clock. Finally, the meal was concluded, and Lottchen was announced. She was asked to say: what is her name? who is she? that was the answer. "I am poor August’s daughter!" "Oh, I know that wretch <note>original: "Aas" - literally 'carrion', a very harsh and dehumanizing insult</note> already," cried the gracious lady in the inner room, "she should clear off, otherwise the servants will whip her away." Disconsolate, she walked through the alleys. She thought of her poor father. "I am very hungry," he had said when I left. How hungry he must be now, and I bring him no help, no comfort! It was already growing dark, and Lottchen <note>A diminutive form of Charlotte, common in the 18th and 19th centuries</note> dared to ask some passersby for alms. Twice she stretched out her trembling hand in vain; the third time, a kind-hearted woman was just about to give her something when a beadle <term>Bettelvogt: a low-ranking official tasked with policing beggars and driving them out of town</term> sprang out from a corner, snatched the gift away from her, and beat the poor girl terribly. A quarter-year's wages were sufficient to buy him an old coat and some wood. Already old August began to trust in Providence <note>The original German "Vorsicht" here refers to Divine Providence or the protective care of God.</note>, when Lottchen’s mistress entered her chamber one day. <meta>The previous page likely detailed the growing tension or the discovery of August's locked room; the catchword "Auf" (On/Open) leads into the opening of the door.</meta>

...no one would open it for her. Finally, she grew fearful and begged two journeymen shoemakers, who were standing right by the stairs, to help her open the door. They burst the decayed door apart and saw that poor August had hanged himself against the wall. Lottchen screamed piteously; the shoemakers went away, and the people in the house gathered around the inconsolable girl. The court officials arrived and found it very suspicious that the dead August had a wound on his head; everyone declared him murdered when they found that he was not hanging, but kneeling, and that even the garter around his neck was quite loose. Half an hour later, Lottchen was dragged to prison, and as they led her across the street, the common people called her a parricide <note>A person who kills their father or a close relative.</note>. Doctors examined her father's corpse, and their testimony agreed with the observations of the court officials. During the interrogation, Lottchen appealed to the testimony of her neighbor, the shoemaker. He and all his journeymen were summoned, and they all swore that they were not present at the opening of the door. She was thus convicted by witnesses of attempting to lie to and deceive her judges, and they condemned her to the torture <note>The use of judicial torture to extract a confession was a standard, though brutal, part of the legal process in the 18th century.</note>.

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>The previous page ended with the catchword "Soon," leading into this account of Lottchen's trial and suffering.</meta>

In the whole, wide world, there was likely never a more unhappy girl than Lottchen! All night long she lay upon her knees; her prayer to God was fervent and urgent. Often despair struggled within her, but just as often, God’s goodness refreshed her with comfort. On the following day, the victim was truly led to the rack <note>original: "Folterbank" — a torture device used to extract confessions</note>, and being unable to endure such pain, she confessed to everything her judges demanded to know. "Is it not true that you murdered your father so that you would no longer have to provide for him?" the stern judge asked her. With eyes turned toward heaven, from which bloody tears trickled, she swore that she had loved her father tenderly and would gladly sacrifice her own life to bring him back to life. But when the executioner seized her, raging within her flesh with his cruel instruments, the agony of death forced a "Yes" from her to every question. A "Yes" that any heart not entirely devoid of feeling would have taken for the loudest "No"! But "Yes" she said, and "Yes" was recorded in the official transcript. Why then was her pleading for God’s help not also recorded? Her anxious prayer to the merciful Savior <note>original: "Erlöser" — referring to Jesus Christ</note>, which forced tears from every eye and awakened in every heart the suspicion: Lottchen must be innocent!

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Lottchen's final days following her trial, maintaining the somber yet reverent tone of the previous account.</meta>

Soon after, her sentence was pronounced. With the most cheerful and calm expression, she heard that her head and hand were to be cut off, and her body was to be placed upon the wheel <note>The "breaking wheel" was a brutal form of capital punishment used in early modern Europe for capital crimes.</note>. The clergyman did not need to comfort her; she was already comforted. With longing, she looked forward to the day of her death, the end of her suffering. Not one of the many hundreds who visited her in her final hours can say that she grumbled about the judgment of her judges; only to her confessor did she entrust the secret of her innocence, and she begged him to tell her mother of her fate after her death.

When she heard the death bell tolling, she said: "That is the bell that calls me to rest!" When the guards came to collect her, she was already standing at the door. Quickly and joyfully she went down the stairs, but when the entire crowd received her with weeping, she wept along with them. As she arrived at the place of execution, she knelt down and prayed silently. After this, she sat down upon the chair herself and asked them to delay no longer! The entire multitude sobbed. "I thank you," said the half-transfigured woman, "I am not unworthy of your compassion, and if God, before whose throne I now appear, is a just God, then he will, sooner or later, reveal my innocence." — — The executioner drew the sword, and Lottchen was no more.

The execution site was still smoking with her innocent blood when two breathless men presented themselves before the court. They were the two journeymen shoemakers who had helped her break open the door. They explained that they were just about to travel away from the city and were waiting in the vestibule for a comrade when Lottchen stepped out of her room. They clearly noticed that her father was still watching her and only bolted the door once she was already on the stairs. They remained standing in the vestibule during Lottchen’s absence and heard a heavy fall in August’s <note>August is Lottchen's father, whose sudden death led to her being wrongfully accused of murder.</note> room. This prompted them to break open the door for her when she returned, and they found the old man already dead. Immediately afterward, they traveled onward because their comrade arrived, and they wished to be in a neighboring city by that evening. They had worked there the entire time and only yesterday evening heard Lottchen’s story and her death sentence being recounted. They had run through the entire night to save the innocent girl and wept bitterly when they heard they had come too late. They swore to their statement under oath and went away in sorrow!

At that height, a broken nail remained stuck, which presumably snapped off when August chose it for his purpose. This explains the wound on his head and the sound of the fall that was heard. The deceased Lottchen was now acquitted <note>original: "losgesprochen" — legally cleared of the suspected crime of infanticide or suicide, allowing for a Christian burial</note>, and her body was buried in the city church. Never have I seen a more beautiful, but also never a sadder funeral procession!

Whenever I remember her story, I often begin to argue with Providence <note>original: "Vorsicht" — referring to Divine Providence or the way God governs the world</note>; but when I reflect again, it becomes luminous and clear to me that God allowed her to bleed so innocently only so that He could prepare for her there <note>referring to the afterlife</note> the most beautiful, most glorious reward for her childlike love.

Amalie’s further fate is unknown to me. A friend told me recently that during the last general famine <note>original: "Theurung" — a period of high prices and scarcity</note>, she lived in poverty and misery in a small town. He himself saw her begging for alms at the gates, and heard upon his departure that she lay ill in the most wretched hut without any help at all.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical or moralizing reflection on the corruption of society versus the purity of rural life, likely following a critique of urban or courtly vice.</meta>

...could be found; such a thing is not to be found here. The poisoned breath of avarice, of faithlessness, and of lies has withered all virtues. The man of honor <note>German: "Biedermann" – an upright, honest citizen of integrity</note> stands alone on earth. He resembles those plants which, transplanted under foreign skies, sink their heads powerlessly to the earth because they lack the necessary nourishment.

Beneficent sleep reigns in the cottage of the laborer and the shepherd. It is interrupted neither by the shrill cry of a guilty conscience, nor by those hideous monsters—hatred, faithlessness, revenge—nor by the vain schemes of avarice and ambition. Innocence and peace reign there. The song of the inhabitants of the air <note>A poetic reference to birds</note> will soon awaken these blessed mortals again. It seems to me that I see how they gaze upon the youthful dawn with looks intoxicated by joy. Their renewed strength circulates with vigor through their rested limbs and stirs in them the need to work. Soon they spread out upon the fields and, smiling, take up their useful labors once more. How could they remain without the liveliest outbursts of gratitude when they hear all of nature celebrating the return of the light?

Beside these essays, the following remarks were also found in Aglaia's writing desk.

Ah! how the harmony of virtue has been corrupted! The most beautiful tones of nature change upon your lips into the howling of dogs.

It would be so beautiful to live in this garden, so pleasant to slumber in this bower, if only the wicked adders were not there, if only the loathsome toads did not sit beneath this rose bush.

Let me embrace you, brother! Let me feel the bliss upon your bosom that I am a human being. But why is it so hard? Why does a suit of armor cover your heart?

O gentle maiden! What do I read in your eye! Is not virtue the beauty of the soul? Are you beautiful? — But why this dissimulation? Take the mask from your face; be simple <note>The original German "einfältig" here implies a sense of guileless innocence or simplicity, rather than foolishness.</note> like nature, artless like the violet in the valley.

# Page 31

What is beauty? It is the harmony of the body with a noble soul. Oh, gentle visionary! Who knows the treasures of nature as well as you do? But where shall I seek you out? — Is the region of Eden not overgrown with thistles? Where is the entrance to the souls of angels? I see only the children of Eve — here — are you perhaps in the grave, most noble of the daughters of men! If so, I will seek you there.

Your eye is gentle, like the eye of the turtledove, and your voice is pleasant, like the voice of the lark in the morning. You resemble the rose in tenderness, and the lily in the purity of your soul; I wish to dwell with you, even if it be within the vaults <note>original: "Krüften," an archaic variant of "Grüften" meaning tombs or crypts</note> of decay; our hours will flow more pleasantly in the halls of death than in the salons of noisy dances, where folly reigns and deception <note>original: "Gaukeley," referring to frivolous trickery or illusory behavior</note> drives the girls around in circles.

Let us dwell with death and be content; he is gentle. Give thanks to him if he will never part us; — give thanks to him, the friend of humanity! —

Precious feeling of nature, you are the mother of these divine joys! They intoxicate the heart of him who, in his conscience, holds the most holy <note>The text cuts off mid-word; "heilig" likely continues as "heiligsten" (most holy) on the following page.</note>

Hail to the man who can return to his father’s old age the care that the father bore for his childhood; hail to the man who incessantly occupies himself with care for his wife and children, dedicating every moment of his life only to them; who presses a tender wife and beloved children to his heart, glowing with love. Though unjust fortune may snatch all his possessions from him; though flames may consume his dwelling, he defies both misfortune and flames. Upon his shoulders he takes his feeble father, in his arms his wife and children; laden with these precious burdens, he pierces through the flames and carries them far from danger. With a hideous crash his house collapses; he hardly turns his head; he has saved all his true treasures. Bread, which his own drops of sweat have procured for him; he distributes it to his father, to his wife, and to his children. A single moment compensates him for the hardships of an entire day; he rests, and then he hastens to earn new joys for himself.

Happy creatures, let me enjoy the spectacle of your bliss for a moment; let me draw lessons in wisdom and virtue from among you. What a thick veil your tenderness throws over the infirmities that cling to human nature! How forbearance among you dries the tears of repentance! Never do the missteps of the children awaken the rage of the father or the mother; never does the sadness of the husband cloud the joy of the wife. Ceaselessly occupied with anticipating one another's needs, providing relief, and mutually forgiving one another, the weaknesses of one person only enhance the brilliance of the virtues of the other. Tender reproaches stream from her eye, mingling with maternal tears. She lets her head fall upon the breast of the guilty daughter and sobs: "Unhappy girl, I shall die of grief!" She can say no more. These words break the daughter's heart; a stream of tears pours from her eyes, she presses her mother into her arms and cries: "O dear mother! weep no more! I, the cause of your death? Ah! only forgive me this once! Never, no, never will I . . . ." Compassionately the mother gazes at her, sees her repentance, yields one of her hands to her, and never again shall her maternal heart bleed over the same offense.

Does one see such heart-melting scenes in the palaces of the great? Does tender feeling seek out hearts beneath those gilded ceilings? These proud palaces open. Oh, how different is the spectacle! Knowing neither duties nor their sweetness, she occupies herself majestically with the great work of her finery <note>original: "Anputz" – refers to elaborate dressing and grooming</note>; with great zeal, her maidservants pile up the pompous edifice of her hair. Hours are scarcely enough for this great labor. A sound is heard; someone is coming; the fire of joy sparkles from her eyes. Is it your husband, O matron <note>original: "Hausmutter" – literally "house-mother," a title implying domestic authority and virtue</note>, is it your children coming to enjoy your embraces? Alas! Meanwhile, those innocent creatures who owe their lives to you arrive. Yet riches do not always harden the heart; they provide the soulful spirit who possesses them with a thousand blissful delights. Klinias <note>A character name likely drawn from Socratic dialogues, representing a person of status and potential virtue</note>, I have seen you press your children to your heart! ...could this sorrowful existence help me? Justice is obtained only through money, and in that, I would be found wanting. I would perish from need before I could even drag myself to the threshold of the courtroom, before I was capable of making my voice audible to the judges.

Lovely and gracious solitude, you have let me taste true bliss; beneath your holy shadow, I have learned to tear myself away from that swarm of needs that made me a slave; here, I have learned to see that I am but a human being, a weak animal whose pride is ridiculous, whose joys consist only of fleeting sensory feelings and the blissful sensations produced by the enthusiasm for virtue. I have experienced that lies and deceit have corrupted the hearts of almost all men, that their company is false and treacherous, and that one must flee from them if one does not wish to be a partner in their injustices; I have learned that only the pleasure of loving and doing good still exists on earth; that the pleasure of being loved is but a chimera <note>original: "Hirngespinnst" — a phantom or figment of the imagination</note> that almost always deceives the unfortunate souls who surrender themselves to it; and that forbearance <note>original: "Nachsicht" — also translated as indulgence or leniency</note> is the first of all virtues, and death the greatest of all goods.

Yes, so it had to be: that black, loathsome scene, a dwelling for beings created for misery. A brief masquerade <note>original: "Mummerey"</note> of gilded joy had to alternate with all the terrors of the abyss, so that even our pleasures—embittered by the impending blow—still prove the hostility of Nature even when she flatters us. Oh, I do her no injustice! What is all our bliss <term>Glückseligkeit: a state of supreme happiness or blessedness</term> if the very next moment carries Death in its arms? Who does not tremble, like Damocles, before the royal feast when abysses open beneath him and swords threaten from above? <note>A reference to the "Sword of Damocles," a Greek parable illustrating the constant peril faced by those in positions of power.</note>

The claim to happiness, equal in all men, begins at birth and lasts until the grave. As little as we feel it when it lies in our hand, just as little do we cast the desired prize away from us; we weep when it flees, and we weep most, value it most, and love it most when it is gone. Thus the favorite falls deeper the higher he was raised; thus the torture stings more cruelly after a period of recovery.

But does our dearest wish not often go astray? Does it not often throw away true happiness for an imaginary one, or at least reach for

An Emperor <note>Likely a reference to the Roman Emperor Honorius, who reportedly grieved more for his pet chicken, Roma, than for the city of Rome itself.</note> despises Rome without effort, yet wept like a fool for a hen; and Lady ** would, like him, not weep to trade her worthy husband for a parrot. Are humans entitled to judge happiness, when it is so little understood and so little valued?

O happiness! <note>original: "Glückseligkeit" — a term implying a state of blessedness or supreme bliss beyond mere luck.</note> Holy, offended name! Shine once, just once, upon my sorrowful life; like a prisoner, I see your light through a crack in the rock, and then sink deeper into the night of my misery.

All worship the beautiful deity, yet few have known her. One seeks her at the plow, another beneath the crown; fools seek her in gold, the wise in the tranquility of the soul; I, most deceived of all, sought her upon the breast of a friend. Earnest wisdom guided my straying thoughts aright and—did not give—but taught what true happiness is. Happiness is (so the goddess taught by the grave of my departed friend, to still my tears—yet they are not stilled!) happiness is that blessed state where you desire no more than what you have. Every thought that wanders beyond that narrow boundary <note>original: "Gränze" (modern: Grenze)</note> gives nothing more, but also disturbs what you already possess. Within the boundary, all is peace; beyond it, wound—

<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion on the nature of misery and the restlessness of the human mind; this page continues that meditation on the impossibility of satisfying human desire.</meta>

...misery, like a besieged army during a sally, drives away every wandering thought. If you wish for no happiness to be lacking from your desires, then desire nothing that can be taken from you! —

I said it before: I shall fall back into my dungeon of misery. Alas! that terrible decree casts me even deeper. — Who can teach me to tame the thunder, or who can hold the storming sea in check? How shall I make peace among the elements, or steady the wavering center of the earth? I could more easily dampen the rebellion of the waves than the rebellion of my heart. If one desire is suppressed, ten thousand more awaken; and the wise man and the fool both die with a wish upon their lips.

You heroes and sages whom the world worships, do not boast; the foolish Midas <note>In Greek mythology, King Midas was granted the power to turn everything he touched into gold, which led to his near-starvation.</note> did no less than you. He starved in golden deprivation out of a love for gold; out of a love for honor, the Cynic <note>A reference to Diogenes of Sinope, the philosopher who lived in a tub (or large jar) to demonstrate his rejection of worldly comforts.</note> rolled in his barrel, and the power-hungry Caesar died. Which was wiser: the love of gold or the love of glory?

In our souls—whether we are fools or sages—there reigns an instinct, a despotic passion; it never yields, even when all others are conquered. It rages without limits, building wish upon...

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>The previous page ended with the catchword "Aus" (From), suggesting a continuation of a philosophical or dramatic monologue regarding the human condition.</meta>

...a wish that, despite reason, torments, desires, burns, repents, and rejoices when it is flattered, yet rages where it cannot prevail. — How, in this turmoil, am I to ask slow-moving reason whether the stars approve or deny my desires? Where is the magic art that can still raging waves with a single word or a sour look? Ah! Whoever demands that I wish for no happiness other than what I can possess, says clearly that there is none to be had!

But would there even be a great man—more than a man—the gentle, the strong, the firm one, whose steadfast soul would yield to no passion, whose eye no error would cloud? One who, under the shield of free virtue <note>The author uses "Tugend" (virtue) here in the classical sense of moral excellence and strength of character.</note>, would walk with a sure step through the tangled paths of life, deaf to the song of the Sirens <note>original: "Syrenen"; a reference to the seductive creatures from Greek mythology who lured sailors to their doom.</note>? A man whose every wish reason would endorse, whose desire would never go further than his own strength—what then? When sickness, when shame, when want intrude, will he deny a tear to his melting eye, conquer the riot of his struggling soul, and boast of calmness amidst the storms? No; in spite of his pride, a tear will tell the truth, a treacherous sigh will break through his boasting lips, and the forced wrinkles on his brow, like smoke from a fire, will bear witness to his suffering. —

<summary>This page explores the conflict between human passion and stoic reason, questioning whether a truly "perfect" man can remain unmoved by suffering and desire. --- Page 52 ---
From all these various essays found in Aglaï's room, one could easily draw conclusions about his temperament <note>original: "Gemüthsart" – referring to his inner disposition or character</note>. His heart was extremely sensitive, and he was destined to be all the more unhappy the more intensely he felt.

A person with too sensitive a heart is, for the world as it is, like an unfortunate plant; he grows like a flower on sandy soil, where bare rocks tower over it and deprive it of every drop of the heavenly dew of human joy; he can do nothing but suffer and die—this is his destiny—his lot. Aglaï's true life story serves as proof. He was a good man, and despite his faults, always worthy of the memory of the righteous. He made no one unhappy but himself, if one can call him unhappy who becomes the victim of his too harmoniously tuned heart.

# Aglaï's True Life Story.

---

I was raised in the countryside; Nature was my teacher; the flower my first book;

<summary>This page introduces the character of Aglaï through his writings, describing him as a tragically sensitive soul whose temperament was ill-suited for the harshness of the world. It begins his "True Life Story," noting his rural upbringing and connection to nature.</summary>
<keywords>Aglaï, sensitivity, temperament, nature as teacher, biography, melancholy</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
book; on its leaves I read that there is a God. The balsamic scents of spring, the swaying seas of grain fields in summer, convinced me of his goodness and told me that he wishes his creatures well. The linnet and the finch, the blackbird and the nightingale instructed me in philosophy, and told me that he who has the fewest needs is one of the happiest among created beings. Since the sun always shone again after the rain, and since joy descended to the earth after the thunderstorm, the thought of a terrifying deity never occurred to me, but only the thought that the Godhead must be entirely love. When the stars were in the sky of an evening, my good father often showed me these countless dwellings; I rejoiced to look at them, and often longed to get to know the brothers who might live there. In my natural concepts I was strengthened by a book from which my father often read to me, and which he called the Gospels. The seal of the Godhead was stamped upon it, and human bliss <note>original: "Menschenseligkeit," referring to the state of spiritual happiness or salvation for mankind</note> was the concept of its teachings. Love God and man—this was the entire system of ethics <note>original: "Sittenlehre," a term for moral philosophy or a code of conduct</note> that my father gave to me. He was no lover of disputing and quarreling, for he told me that he is not wise who speaks much,

Son, you are a human being and live among
human beings; Nature knows nothing but humans;
whether white or black, brown or copper-colored; —
whether they wear purple smocks or linen ones.
There is nothing but humans in the world; the
man in the turban, the man in the synagogue
is a human being, just as you are; you have no 
privilege over him, he is a child of the Deity, just as
you are; what pleases you, also pleases him, what
pains you, also pains him; he is You yourself;
treat him as yourself.

There are animals in the world; man
says that he is the noblest among them — or could
be, I would rather say. — Animals
exist, and man is therefore, in respect
to his physical structure, also an animal being.
That which is necessary for the maintenance of this animal being
is called "needs." You will
realize, they are few in Nature. There
are necessary needs; there are imaginary 
needs.

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with the catchword "and," leading into this discussion on human needs and the nature of existence.</meta>

The satisfaction of the imaginary needs <note>The author distinguishes between "imaginary" luxuries and "necessary" survival needs.</note> of a few individuals has displaced the satisfaction of the necessary ones for more than half of the human race; hence, there is so much misery in humanity.

Enjoy the pleasures of the world like a confection; do not grow accustomed to them, otherwise you will be unhappy.

Nothing is permanent except what exists within the essence of nature itself; everything else is fleeting—perishable.

The apple is an apple; the plum, a plum; the wolf, a wolf; the lamb, a lamb; man is man; learn to know man as he is, not as he ought to be.

You cannot make a plum out of an apple; a lamb out of a wolf; nor an angel out of a man.

Learn from these principles to rejoice in the good of every being, and do not dwell upon the evil, without which perhaps the good would not exist.

Learn in this world, good Aglais! <note>Aglais is likely a pseudonym or a direct address to a student/initiate, common in Enlightenment-era philosophical dialogues.</note> to be content with little; learn to drink water, to digest crab apples <note>original: "Holzäpfel" — wild, sour apples, signifying the simplest and harshest of diets.</note>, to go half-naked in poor rags, to live without a house, and to sleep on stones—

and you will get through in this way; however, you must also be able to do without a friend, to forget a beloved, to look a scoundrel in the face without becoming angry, and to admire a fool’s cap <note>The "Narrenkappe" or fool's cap symbolizes the vanity and absurdity of high-ranking but incompetent officials.</note> without laughing. If you can do all this, and if you dispute with no theologian and quarrel with no lawyer <term>Juristen (lawyer/jurist)</term>, then you will presumably make your way. You must be like a snail; be content with your little house and pull in your horns: but since I know you to be too sensitive <term>empfindsam (sensitive/sentimental)</term>, I always fear bad things for you.

You are a good boy, Aglais! <note>Aglais is a Greek name meaning "splendor" or "brightness," here used for the recipient of this moral counsel.</note> but forgive me that I raised you as a lamb among the wolves; I should have formed you to be less sensitive.

But no! even if the flower withers from the breath of the north wind, it was still a more beautiful gift of nature for the gardener than the thistle; simply die, if you cannot endure the harsh winds; it is better to die as a flower than to disfigure the garden as a thistle.

You will have much to endure, Aglais! you are good and also weak, for from the weak heart comes the good: but soon the weak can again become the good; — be on your guard!! —

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>Continuity from page 48: The author continues his psychological advice on the cultivation of the soul and the management of one's internal emotional state.</meta>

Because you are good, your nerves are more finely tuned; your passions must necessarily be more vehement.

Do not let any bungler <note>German: "Pfuscher," referring to an unskilled or careless worker</note> play upon the instrument of your soul, otherwise the strings will snap in two: — it will become difficult to deal with you.

Dilettantes <note>German: "Stümpler"</note> ruin the harmony; and how few virtuosos there are when it comes to human feeling.

You have less to fear from the stronger passions, such as anger, vengefulness, hatred, envy, pride — than from the gentler ones. Through your upbringing, your nervous constitution was not tuned to the impressions of the former; they will bring no tones into the flute of your soul; but friendship, love, and enthusiasm <note>German: "Schwärmerey," which in the 18th century implied a state of excessive, visionary, or fanatical emotion</note> will be dangerous for you; — if only they produce no false notes. These passions know masterfully how to play upon soft instruments; happy are you, if only the harmony is not disturbed: though it cannot happen without some false notes occurring at times.

Do not spoil yourself by reading books into seeing everything differently than it truly is; be rather a poor original than an excellent copy.

Know that most moralists <note>German: "Sittenlehrer," teachers of ethics or customs</note> copied one from another without knowing themselves. Believe the rigorists <note>Those who adhere to strict, inflexible moral rules</note> least of all, for the proof is clear that they knew themselves the worst of all.

<summary>The author warns a sensitive individual to protect their emotional "harmony" from unskilled influencers and cautions that gentle passions like love are more dangerous to a refined soul than violent ones. --- Page 58 ---
Man is weak; he judges with the strength of philosophy and virtue regarding the past and the future; but the present he forgets: philosophers for the most part possess more theory than practice.

It is difficult to combine theory with practice, for in theory, the spirit and the intellect are at work; in practice, it is the heart and the will that must act.

When we judge a person who has lost his way in a swampy region, we—who are standing on dry ground—dictate every step he should have taken, and do not consider that a person in the delirium of passion can easily lose his senses.

It is cruel to treat a deaf man poorly because he does not hear, or to strike a blind man when he bumps his head; it is just as cruel to show oneself so intolerant toward the faults of our neighbor; for one can sometimes also be deaf in the soul and blind in the heart.

The wise always dictate with oracular pronouncements <note>original: "Orakelsprüche"; refers to authoritative, often cryptic or unquestionable decrees</note> how other people should act, and do not place us in the situation in which they are acting. Give the one who errs your upbringing, your nervous constitution <note>original: "Nervenbau"; a contemporary 18th-century term referring to one's physical temperament or biological makeup</note>, your circumstances, and he will act—

Who can judge the hearts of men? No one but God; to Him alone are the inner powers of man known.

Be indulgent toward everyone: for when one walks on a slippery path, it is easy to fall. Do not laugh at the misstep of your neighbor, for you are as close to a fall as he is. One has much to do before one can stand securely on the ice-skates of philosophy: a small pebble has often thrown the most secure philosophical skater to the ground. <note>The author uses "ice-skates" (Eisschuhen) as a metaphor for the precarious nature of intellectual certainty.</note>

The human heart has a thousand folds; the one closest to a fall is he who believes himself furthest from it.

Learn to recognize the fool by the stubbornness of his expressions; the simpleton by the reliability <note>In this context, "reliability" implies an unearned or arrogant confidence in one's own opinions.</note> of his judgments; and the wise man by his mistrust of himself.

I found these drafts of principles among my father's papers; it seems to me he already sensed my fate. I augmented the truths of his sentences with my own; I wish to set them down here: perhaps many a sensitive, unfortunate soul can find comfort for their wounds.

Man is like a plant; the place where it grows; the circumstances under which it sprouts; the juices of the soil that nourish it; the mixture of the earth's components determine its taste and its quality. Thus man grows, cast by fate into a region like the seed of a spruce tree chased from the tree by the wind; — he becomes what temperament and education form out of him: a rogue — or a coward. Thus our crowned virtue is often the plaything of the temperaments; a meteor that arises when the good side prevails, and disappears again in an instant when another mixture <note>The "mixture" refers to the balance of bodily fluids or humors believed to dictate personality.</note> gains the upper hand.

Temperaments incline a person toward action; but reason should guide them; yet sometimes reason is the master and leads the way; at other times it is the lackey, following behind and carrying the train of Folly's gown.

I would like to know of a single year where there were no storms, where no north wind howled, and no thunderstorm threatened death and destruction; but I would also like to know the man who is always calm, always himself, equally strong, equally a powerful master of his passions; — one is as impossible as the other.

Friend! there stand four poor earthly creatures together; they move me to the very soul. A soul, destined to live for me; a soul adorned with every charm of virtue, with tenderness, with open kindness, so mild, so good, so beyond all praise; mortals were bound to adore her, and her sister angels to love her. — Oh you! eternally beloved, eternally venerable friend! Alas! since I have been separated from your all-animating gaze, my day has been a torment and my night a hell! — Oh, I wish with sighs that I had never known the value of friendship in lonely forests, but had instead stared out at worlds around me as if at the barren rocks of Arabia! Cruel, but just, since society only weaves bonds in order to tear them apart all the more heartlessly.

The impulse of movement had to be strong; activity is its destiny; it incites, drives, and enlivens. Reason had to be calm and still; destined only to restrain, to deliberate, to advise. Self-love <note>German: *Selbstliebe*; in this 18th-century context, it refers to the natural instinct for self-preservation and immediate desire</note> is stronger because its objects lie near at hand; those of reason lie in the distance. The former sees immediate good through the senses; the latter sees future good in the consequences. Temptations press in more powerfully than reasons; at best, reason is merely more vigilant, while self-love is stronger! — Use your reason to tame the stronger force, and listen only to her. Attentiveness provides practice and experience; each strengthens reason and weakens self-love.

Let brooding pedants lead both friends into the field of battle, more concerned with separating than with uniting them; as soon as they quarrel, all charm, all virtue, all the quick dexterity of wit, sense, and reason fall away! — Scholars bicker like fools, often over a name, and in the end mean nothing or the very same thing.

Self-love and reason have the same purpose: an abhorrence of evil and a desire for well-being. But the one is insatiable and would devour everything—

The passions are nothing but the determination of self-love. True or apparent well-being sets them in motion. But since not everything good can be for everyone, and reason commands everyone to care for themselves; so too is selfish passion, if its paths are correct, a friend of reason and worthy of its care: if it spreads its bounty to others, it rises to the rank of virtues.

Let the Stoic <note>A follower of the ancient Greek school of philosophy that taught the endurance of pain or hardship without the display of feelings and without complaint.</note> boast in sluggish insensitivity; his virtue is frozen solid—all shrunken into the heart. Strength of the soul is movement, not rest. The surging storm drives the soul to work, perhaps disturbing parts, but preserving the whole. We sail back and forth on the wide sea of life; reason is the compass, but passion is the wind. Even God is not always in His stillness—He ascends upon the storm and travels upon the wind.

Passions are, like the elements, born for struggle; but mixed and tempered, they unite in His work. It is enough to temper and use them; but what the human—

Enough! **Reason** follows the path of **Nature**, tames it, compares it—follow it and **God**!

Love, hope, joy, the laughing retinue of pleasure; hatred, fear, pain, the people of suffering, mixed with wisdom, and bound within their limits, establish and maintain the equilibrium of the soul, mixing light and shadow, and giving life strength and color!

Pleasure lies always in our eye or hand, ceases in enjoyment, and lives in the prospect. To snatch the present, to seek the future, is the eternal business of the body and the soul. All display their charms, but not all charm; every sense has its particular objects: therefore the impression is different, strong or weak, like the organ upon which it acts; and therefore a **ruling passion** <note>original: "Hauptleidenschaft"; the primary drive that dominates an individual's character</note> hovers above all, and swallows them all, like **Aaron's serpent**! <note>A biblical reference to Exodus 7:12, where Aaron’s rod turned into a serpent and swallowed the serpents of the Egyptian magicians.</note>

Nature is the mother of mighty passion; practice nourishes it; wit, spirit, and talents all make it worse; even reason often gives it sharpness and strength, just as the sun's ray turns wine sour!

## Life and Death.

Life is a small, muddy, and unhealthy island, which separates us from the immeasurable mainland of Nature.

The most charming objects contained on this island are like the dust of summer, and even its inhabitants are like the leaves of autumn.

There is no day that does not reveal a secret to a man who thinks, which makes life even more loathsome to him.

->* * *<-

Life is similar to the moon; dark in itself, but shining through reflection.

--- Page 72 ---
Life, as an end in itself, is of no value; but as a means, it cannot be esteemed highly enough. Unfortunate end! Heavenly means! If it is our everything, then it is nothing. If we value it as nothing, then it is our everything.

If there were no death, then humanity would live in vain: if there were no death, even the foolish would wish to die.

My death provides the <term>defense</term> <note>original: "Schutzschrift" – a legal or formal written defense or apology</note> for my birth. Without the former, would the latter not be a curse?

The cries of childhood and the agonies of death; a light tribute which Nature pays for us: and the value of each is a life.

Who can either prolong my life or shorten my existence? The arm of an angel could not preserve me from the grave: legions of angels could not maintain me in this life.

--- Page 74 ---
Where have so many mighty nations gone? — We rarely consider them from this dark perspective, even though their epitaphs <note>original: "Grabschriften"</note> constitute half of our scientific knowledge.

What dust has not once been alive? The shovel and the plow disturb our ancestors.

Merry children of the world! You eat pulverized corpses: you drink extracted human juices, you dance upon dead nations.

The ornaments which most delight the eyes—the statues, the precious tapestries, the paintings—show the mortality of man no less than mourning scaffolds <note>referring to the temporary wooden structures built for elaborate funeral ceremonies</note> and magnificent tombs.

Our very amusements speak to us of death. We pull heroes out of their graves; we let them mount the stage to entertain us. We admire or pity their fate in order to forget our own.

Our parents, by leaving the world, increase our income. We live sumptuously, like other worms, at the expense of the dead.

->* * *<-

For the greater part of humanity, "passing the time" is called living: but will dying also be a pastime for them?

Death imprisons the body: life imprisons the soul; and there is no day where the soul does not have to pay very dearly for such a wretched dwelling.

Death humbles the rich, the noble, and the conqueror. Life humbles man.

Death has apparent evils which Nature does not notice: life has real evils which set even Wisdom itself in astonishment.

Death has no true terror of which life is not the cause: life has no true joys which death does not increase.

<meta>The previous page concluded with the catchword "True," likely leading into a discussion on the true nature of humanity versus false education.</meta>

Should I call the vulture a turtledove to a child, and describe the wolf as a lamb? — Terrible deception! — and yet so commonplace in our education! — They lie to us about a human being whom Nature does not know; they turn our own heart into a structure of imagination, and thus the human grows up, wanting to become an angel, and becomes a Satan, because they have not taught him how to become a human. I investigated the secret impulses of my own heart, studied my fellow man impartially, and found that man is a good being, and that nothing but self-deception makes him unhappy. Self-deception, which the self-interest <note>original: "Eigennuz"</note> of the stronger approves, the stupidity of the weaker idolizes, and the exhaustion of those oppressed by prejudices slavishly reveres.

Place a man such as I was, with my principles, with my heart, with the fiery imagination, with the vivid feelings, into the various situations of life that I shall record here, and judge impartially upon them. What will become of him? — First a philanthropist <note>original: "Menschenfreund," literally a friend of humanity</note>, then an unhappy sufferer, who, if he does not succumb in the storm, finally transforms into a misanthrope <note>original: "Menschenfeind," literally an enemy of humanity</note>, who must either bury himself in a wasteland or despair.

<vocab>Education, Imagination, Self-deception, Self-interest, Prejudices, Philanthropist, Misanthrope, Wasteland</vocab>

<summary>The author critiques traditional education for creating false expectations of human nature, arguing that self-deception leads a good-hearted person to eventually become a misanthrope. He proposes an impartial study of his own life's struggles to illustrate this psychological transformation.</summary>
<keywords>education, human nature, self-deception, philanthropy, misanthropy, prejudice, psychology, nature vs nurture</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
True philosophy is to know oneself, humanity, and the world as they are; — not as they ought to be; not as we wish them to be; not as they are depicted to us in books. The chemist <note>original: "Scheidekünstler" – literally a "separation artist," an early term for a chemist or alchemist who separates pure metals from ore</note> who can prudently purify gold from the dross is a philosopher. I could not achieve it; I succumbed — I died.

This book, which I now leave behind, shall be a good book for many. One will perhaps not yet understand the introduction, nor the sentences and excerpts cited thus far; but in the sequence of things, everything will become clear. May people learn to become wise from my misfortune!

What has happened to me, I do not wish to recount in a tedious manner; here is my notebook. Let one read of my accidents; judge the impression they made upon me, and study humanity from this, and learn to be wiser.

...should. Friend, I have experienced so much that I can almost say: everything has passion as its motive; everything is a lie, nothing is truth. My own history would convince you; but it is too long for our conversation; also, it could be more harmful to your heart from another perspective. The best thing I can tell you is: avoid people as much as you can, enter into few connections, believe no one, and do not trust until you are convinced that the other person is just as strange a human among humans as you are. I pity you; things will go badly for you in the world until you find that I am right. Pray to Heaven that it gives you lynx-eyes <note>original: "Luxenaugen"; a reference to the lynx's legendary keen sight</note>, a dog’s nose, mole’s ears, and a soft step so that no one else hears you walking; then you will see, hear, smell, and sneak up on everything, and learn how people deal with you. Your head will learn to mine and counter-mine <note>military metaphors for digging tunnels to place explosives under an enemy and digging intercepting tunnels to stop them</note>, to learn pressure and counter-pressure; but your heart will feel all of that. I know for certain that you do not believe a word of all this; it is the character of the inexperienced; but nevertheless, it will make you observant when the opportunity arises. Adieu.

->The 22nd of the Rose-Month.<- <note>June</note>

Either there must be incredible villains among mankind who commit the most gruesome shameful deeds, which rouse the most tender, patient heart against humanity; or my old friend is a light-shunning monster among men, to whom everything seems black, every human deed a deed of darkness; who is himself evil enough to shame others as being full of vice. For the sake of humanity, I would prefer to think neither. But I do not understand it; I do not know how to unravel this thing for myself.

I saw and came to know no other people than those sheltered by a lowly hut, and no one had told me that the people in the city were different people than those in the country.

->The 23rd of February.<-
<note>original: "Rosenmond" — an archaic German poetic term for February, literally "Rose Month," though sometimes used for June; context here suggests the late winter/early spring transition.</note>

The strength of a proof is never felt more irresistibly than when history supports it with examples. Should the misanthrope <term>misanthrope: a person who dislikes humankind and avoids human society</term> not cast aside his dark prejudice of loathing all people, simply because perhaps a few base individuals, who abused his trust, deceived him? Should he not learn to think better of his brothers when examples of the noblest deeds convince him of the goodness of better men? Should he not at least believe that there are just as many good, noble people in society as there are villains of the same degree within it? —

Yes, certainly, or else his heart would have to be entirely hardened and closed off to feelings of the noble and the beautiful; the fountain of tears of joy over a touching scene would dry up — his condition would be the most pitiable — the most detestable among men. But such an unfortunate soul does not exist — indeed, cannot exist.

Oh, what a bliss it shall be for me to reconcile a man once more with humanity, to open his heart again to the joy of living among people, so that he learns to feel the value of society even in his gray years.

--- Page 110 ---
Joyfully, I will now record the story of yesterday evening—the most beautiful trait from the history of noble souls—which I wish to tell my old friend once more; and may it spread light over his dark soul.

As amiable as Z*** appeared in my eyes through his noble action and the generosity with which he forgave the ungrateful man and became his benefactor once again, he became even more so yesterday in a manner that will make his heart eternally invaluable to me.

Many years ago, he supported one of his good acquaintances in a most urgent emergency with a not insignificant sum, without having sufficient legal security for it—other than a mere, insignificant <term>promissory note</term> <note>original: "Obligationsschein"; a written document acknowledging a debt without the formal weight of a court-registered bond</note>—and without knowing a certain time for repayment. Reckless and ungrateful, this man used his friend's help, improved his circumstances, and forgot to whom he owed his fortune. — Thus he lived, and left a considerable estate to his son. This son now found this debt indicated in his father's books, but nowhere a further explanation of it. Yet he went to Z***, thinking more nobly than his father, and inquired about the nature of this <term>loan</term> <note>original: "Anlehens"</note>.

The good man lost his composure even more, overcome by Z***’s noble spirit <note>original: "Edelmuth"</note>, than he had previously been driven out of his senses by terror. I will seek out the good youth who thought so honestly of paying off his father's old debt, and I know for certain he will be grateful to me for providing him the opportunity to raise his joy to the highest degree of bliss through doing good.

There, he yielded entirely to his thoughts and felt the bliss of the blessed amidst the scenes of holy Nature. His heart never found joy in the boisterous pleasures of the world. To make people happy was his wish, and to see people suffer was the torment of his soul.

Fremond received this letter from the hands of a well-meaning stranger. This wretched woman became entangled in a lawsuit, and she came to the city where Fremond lived.

She presented her sad situation to him, and Fremond allowed himself to be beguiled. He advanced her money and took her into his house as a friend. It was not enough that she renounced all shame; she also wished to drive the blush of modesty from every honest cheek that was still colored by a thought of integrity. <note>The author uses "Schamröthe" (blush of shame/modesty) to contrast with Blanka's brazen behavior.</note>

Since Blanka had been in the city, the most terrible scenes took place in Fremond's house. People whispered about it in the city; everyone knew of it, except for Fremond, who built upon the virtue of his Therese and the honesty of his sister with blind confidence.

He could not imagine that he was being deceived—he, who deceived no one—he, who meant so well, so cordially and sincerely toward all people; how could he be betrayed? And by whom? By Blanka, whom he loved? By Therese, whom he adored? Fremond did not think such a thing possible.

The thousand-tongued rumor <note>original: "tausendzüngige Gerücht," a classical allusion to Fama, the personification of rumor in Roman mythology</note> finally spread further and reached Fremond's ears; just as the noise of a conflagration reaches the ears of one resting carelessly, who only hears the tumult caused by the flames when they are already crashing down over him.

Fremond. So it is, dear friend; as much as I loved mankind, I strove in vain to win their love in return. I wanted to depend entirely upon a creature who, in turn, depended entirely upon me; but where was I to find such a one? — Friendship, love, marital bliss — what magnificent names! Reineck. You can tell me nothing more that I have not already felt as vividly for you as you could ever feel it yourself.

--- Page 146 ---
Fremond. Why should I have left her, since she was so kind as to compensate me with her pleasant company for the boredom I usually feel at our public amusements? — I do not play cards and I do not dance: You danced, Therese, and enjoyed yourself; so do not begrudge me my joy either.

Therese. No, not at all. — Will you likely keep her company again at the next ball?

Fremond. I intend to, for we promised it to one another.

Therese. Very well! Hm! She may be good-hearted; but beautiful — hm! She is certainly not beautiful. Is it not true that, in this regard, I still deserve the preference?

Fremond. Above all others, Therese! But according to my way of thinking, a good heart is the most powerful charm, and I always view beauty as a secondary matter. You know me, Therese. Beauty is always less dangerous to me than a gentle soul. — Such a creature could draw me entirely to herself. —

Therese. Draw you entirely to herself?

Fremond. Oh, yes!

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page continues the correspondence between Fremond and Reineck, following Fremond's strategic conversation with Therese regarding his emotional detachment and her potential to reclaim his affection.</meta>

Therese: And you could then leave everything that you once loved?

Fremond: It would be possible: however, for a person whom I once loved, it would be very easy to chain me completely to her again. She would only need to adopt this noble, gentle being; this devotion, which charms me so; in a word, that which I so gladly idealize in a woman, and which is so powerful as to captivate my soul—and she would have power over me in all things.

Here, friend! Here you have the entire conversation. What will be the consequence of it? —

I do not doubt that Therese will have understood me. The fear of losing my heart entirely will give her back to me. It already seems to me as if she is wrapping her arms around me, as if tears are rolling from her eyes, as if she is entirely mine again. More on this tomorrow.
