->The 20th of February.<- <note>original: "Rosenmond" (Rose-month), an archaic German name for February.</note>

What a considerable increase my writing tablet receives daily—but truly not to the honor of this over-refined <note>original: "klügelnd," referring to people who try to be overly clever or pedantic</note> race. I believe if people let me live another twenty years, I shall have twenty thick folio volumes that would outweigh all the "library-bellies" <note>A satirical term for large, stuffed books that take up space without offering substance.</note>.

I have so much left over from Z***’s meal—such entirely new, unique features from the gallery of humanity—that I cannot possibly leave them to oblivion. Then I wish to give this collection and Fremond’s history to my good old misanthrope <note>original: "Menschenhasser," literally a man-hater</note> to read, and say to him: Friend! I want to move from this wretched quarter of the earth to another, and if I find the people there just as deceitful, slanderous, perjured, malicious, and base as here, then Friend! we shall leave them all, move into a wilderness, unknown and inaccessible to men; we shall make friends with the bears, and entrust ourselves to the friendly protection of the ravenous wolves.

## The 31st of February.
<note>original: "Rosenmond" — literally "Rose Moon," an archaic German name for February.</note>

"Just listen," Z*** said to me a few days ago, "to the wretched, base trick that has been played on me. I wanted to carry out a plan <term>Plan: a strategic or political scheme</term> that I had been considering for a long time; through it, in addition to considerably increasing my income, I would have been placed in the most advantageous position against my enemies. But to achieve my aim, I directly required the assistance <term>Beystand: formal support or backing</term> of a certain H***—the very same man you saw me speaking with alone recently before dinner. I am familiar with his circumstances, and I knew that I could bind him to my interests with a small sum of money. I did so, and informed him of my project, for which I requested <note>original: "bath" — archaic spelling of "bat," meaning requested or entreated</note> his support. He assured me most solemnly that he would do everything for me, and that he would present the matter in the most brilliant light to the Marquis**, whose favorite he is. I know human nature, and his zeal became suspicious to me. I was therefore quite certain that all his assurances were merely compliments in exchange for my favor, and that he would not take the slightest step on my behalf: but that he should be directly against me, that he should betray <term>verrathen: to betray or reveal a secret</term> me—I did not think the man so shameful as that."

<vocab>February, plan, assistance, Marquis, support, requested, betray</vocab>

->* * *<-

I cannot leave unnoticed another manifestation of humanity, the likes of which I have never seen before. It was a man who, as he was described to me, forces his way into every house, collects the anecdotes of the city, and brings them into social circles travestied <note>meaning parodied or distorted</note> with his own inventions. He is everyone’s most obedient servant and friend; he gives his approval everywhere, praising and blaming just as one wishes it to be; he allows himself to be used for all manner of business and errands. He is religious, devout, unbelieving, or profane, exactly as one wants him to be and as the person with whom he speaks thinks. In assemblies and at the dinner tables, he plays the jester, the fool, and the rogue, and all of this—merely for the sake of his dear midday soup. He knows how to find the dinners <note>original: "Diners," referring to formal afternoon meals</note>

I certainly believe the constituent parts of this human being are entirely homogenous with the constituent parts of a pig's head; otherwise, I could not possibly explain this rare friendship. What people! — No! I can bear them no longer.

->The 1st of June.<- <note>original: "Brachmonat," an archaic German term for June, literally the "fallow month" when fields were plowed.</note>

A few days ago, a young man came to me who formerly, while still a small boy, lived in my birthplace. <note>original: "Geburtsort."</note> His appearance was pitiful, and the description of his fate could be nothing less than a fabrication, as everything about him and within him convinced me of the truth.

I no longer recognized him, and could only faintly remember having seen him once. For it was already a very long time ago. He left that place as a child and was raised in a distant city; his destiny made him a citizen of the world. <note>original: "Weltbürger," a cosmopolitan figure or wanderer.</note> He traveled everywhere and was at home everywhere. The manifold hardships he had endured on his travels and under various climates <note>original: "Climaten."</note> robbed him of his health; he had traveled a great distance on foot; thus, he had scarcely more clothing than he needed for the most necessary covering. Sick, naked, and helpless, I saw him before my door, such that I have never seen a beggar more miserable. While I gathered some pieces of clothing for him, we spoke of his history; <note>original: "Geschichte," meaning his life story or narrative.</note> I also told him for the most part my own, and so we recognized one another.

Z*** seemed very reluctant to do so; yet he did not say no directly! Instead, he held back his decision.

->The 3rd of June<- <note>original: "Brachmonats" – an archaic German term for June, the "fallow month."</note>

Yesterday my old spleen <note>The "spleen" refers to a state of melancholy, irritability, or low spirits, often personified in 18th-century literature as a visiting demon or phantom.</note> returned to me. It was already late in the evening, and I could not imagine what brought him to me at such an hour, all the less so because I had recently encountered him with great vehemence, provoked by his speech. I was startled to see him; for I truly do not like to see him. In hours when I am immersed in a feeling of bliss, imagining human majesty and human happiness; unmindful of evil, my soul occupying itself only with good, upright people; then the fiend <note>original: "Unhold" – a monster or malicious spirit.</note> disputes away my most glorious ideas, startles me out of my sweet dream, and sets the ugliest figures against my ideals, and makes me so dejected, so sad, that I want to cry like a child whose beautiful, golden bubbles are snatched away by a mischievous boy before they can burst in the air.

## Mrs. F —.

"Do not weep," said the dying Mr. F — to his two daughters, "we shall see each other again one day! I die peacefully, as I leave you a tender mother and her fortune, which will allow her to raise you well. Farewell, but not forever! Live so that we may meet again in the hereafter!" He died, and his wife and children mourned him for a long time, for he was a tender husband and a good father! The fortune he left behind consisted of forty thousand <term>Thalers<note>A large silver coin used throughout Europe for centuries; 40,000 was a very substantial sum for a private family.</note></term> in cash, which he had loaned to a nobleman, receiving a large <term>Manorial Estate<note>German: Rittergut. A property traditionally held by a knight or nobleman, carrying specific legal privileges.</note></term> as collateral. This nobleman died shortly thereafter; the estate was sold by the heirs, and Mrs. F — received the entire capital back. However, with this money came a great worry: where and how could she reinvest it profitably? The guardian of her children and many other friends advised her to place it in the bank of the millionaire R —. "It is as safe there as in a public bank, and R — is so magnanimous that he pays six percent interest to every widow who invests her money with him," they all said, and Mrs. F — traveled to see him herself. After long hesitation on his part, and ostensibly only for the benefit of the uneducated <note>The German "unerzogen" here likely refers to the children being "not yet fully raised" or "underage" rather than lacking manners.</note>

orphans, he had the money deposited into his bank. Madame F— thanked him for his kindness and returned home happy and content; for her income had increased by a third; she was now able not only to live without worry for her sustenance <note>original: "Nahrungssorge" – the anxiety of providing basic needs like food and shelter</note>, but also to raise her daughters respectably. The vision of the joy she hoped to experience through her children in her later years sweetened the loss of her dear husband and promised her the brightest future. She could hope for this with good reason, for the beautiful Florchen <note>a diminutive form of Flora</note> was only fourteen years old and already excelled in many feminine arts and sciences. Caroline, a gentle six-year-old girl, seemed poised to surpass even her.

Madame F— had been home for only three days when she received a letter from her lawyer in L—. Scarcely had she read three lines when she sank unconscious from her chair. Her daughters tried in vain to wake her; they gathered neighbors and friends together, who finally called her back to life, and in the fallen letter they read that the wealthy R— had committed a bankruptcy of more than a million and had secretly fled. Mrs. F— saw indeed that it was possible to support herself and her children in this manner; but the loss of her entire fortune, and the wretched state in which she found herself placed by a villain <note>The "villain" likely refers to a legal adversary or a fraudulent debtor mentioned earlier in the narrative</note>, pained her deeply. No amount of time or comfort could soothe her grief; she wept often and much during her work, which required a great strain on the eyes. It followed that, a year later, she could see very little; she grew even more distressed by this, and in the second year, she became entirely blind.

The condition of a blind person is already the most terrible in nature; but it was far more terrible still for poor Mrs. F—, who now certainly believed she and her children would fall victim to hunger, because Florchen was by no means yet a master <term>Künstlerinn: literally "female artist," here referring to a highly skilled professional lace-maker or seamstress</term> in this work, and Karoline could only hem a cloth or knit a stocking. Karoline had contracted smallpox <note>original: "Blattern"</note>. Her constitution was too exhausted to be able to help itself against this dangerous disease, and so she died on the fifth day.

Be at peace, said her landlord the next morning, I will not cast you out of my cottage; you may live here as long as I live, and some counsel may yet be found for your daily sustenance. Go out once or twice a week to beg; if people give to strangers, they will surely give something to you as well! Today is Alms-day <note>A specific day, often Friday or a feast day, when charity was traditionally distributed at the church</note>, try it for once; my little boy will lead you. With all my heart, said little Jacob; he took her hand and led her slowly out of the cottage. Lead me to the church, dear Jacob! said Mrs. F—. They arrived there, but the church was locked, and the poor blind woman prayed upon the threshold for a full hour. As she stood up, she said: There must be a pond near here? "Yes! right down there!" Lead me to it! "What do you want to do there?" I only wish to wash my feet a little! "Well, here we are! Sit yourself down!" She bent down and felt for the water with her hand. Thereupon she stepped back a few paces, broke away from her guide,

and jumped in very quickly. The boy ran weeping to his mother; but before she could tell her neighbor, before any arrangements were made, a whole hour passed, and only after three hours was the dead woman found.

Her old dog, whom I have already mentioned above, was the only one who followed her corpse; he whimpered as they covered her with earth, and starved himself to death upon her grave.

Is this faithful animal not dearer to you, sensitive souls, not more worthy of veneration, than those people who let the poor F— <note>Likely a redacted surname, common in 18th-century literature to suggest a real person</note> starve to death?

This story has now completely unnerved me. How much misery is there not here below! — It is decided: now I want to flee the city again and hide myself in a hut in the country. — This letter, which I am attaching here, has strengthened my resolve.

# Alcest to Aglais.

->The 8th of June.<- <note>original: "Brachmonats," an archaic German name for June, the "fallow month."</note>

Although sadness and tenderness stirred in my heart as my offended Thales <note>A reference to the Greek philosopher, here likely a pseudonym for a friend seeking wisdom.</note> left the city, when I consider his resolution more closely, I praise his choice. I commend the hermit even as I pity the friend who decides to breathe a purer air in remote fields, far from the city and from vice. For who would not rather inhabit the most wretched village, or the most desolate fields, than this crowded and thronged city? There, no sudden death snatches men away; instead, all whom hunger spares die of old age. But here, malice, robbery, and chance conspire together; at one moment the gathered mob rages, at another, a fire. Merciless highwaymen hide here in ambush. Busy fraudsters roam about here after prey; falling houses thunder over our heads, and an effeminate atheist prattles us to death.

While Thales awaited the boat, which [carried] the small remainder of his scattered wealth—

A strange letter, full of misery, full of whimpering against humanity; it seems to me my father once wrote to me on this very point; I will look for his letter and place it here.

Indeed, it is a difficult art, the art of being happy! But do not, for that reason, consider it impossible, my son. There have been people who were happy; and why should there not still be some? The Creator, who formed us all according to one pattern and created us for one purpose, is no more partial toward one generation <note>original: "Geschlecht" - can mean generation, race, or lineage</note> than toward another. Either no one must be happy, or everyone must be able to be so.

I have grown old, my son, and have always found more reason to thank the goodness of my Creator than to complain about His dispensations <note>original: "Schickungen" - refers to divine providence or the twists of fate</note>. ...if you are not so already! You have taken a brief joy, a happy surge of the heart, a dream, for true bliss <term>Glückseligkeit: a state of supreme happiness or blessedness</term>; in the middle of the night, you have mistaken the sudden flash of lightning for the break of day.

But who can be constantly happy? The obstacles are manifold. — When we consider with a settled reason the innumerable accidents to which our life is subject, one should almost marvel, not that man is merely unhappy, but that he can live even a single hour with pleasure. What can not be harmful to us? From without: the world, other people, accidents, want, and even abundance itself; from within: our desires, our passions, our errors, our own hearts, our hatred, and even our philanthropy <note>original: "Menschenliebe" — literally 'love of humanity'</note>. So many steps, so many dangers; a thousand cares, a thousand pains, for a single joy! One is tormented by himself, another by the world, yet another by accidents.

Follow me, my son! with your thoughts. I wish to lead you through a variety of sorrowful scenes, and to place the world before your eyes in a melancholy prospect. When we thus learn to know life; then we begin to realize what we can expect from it. We begin to doubt the common system of happiness; we draft for ourselves an entirely different plan, and we either guard ourselves against accidental afflictions, or, if we cannot prevent them, we at least arm ourselves with a **steadfastness** <note>original: "Standhaftigkeit" — the virtue of remaining firm and resolute in the face of adversity</note> against them.

He who expects a perfect happiness in the world must have looked and thought very little about him, or he must be very short-sighted. Nature, therefore, does not leave us in doubt for long. As children, we stand in connection with only a very small part of the world: but this connection increases with the years, just as our passions, strengths, and needs increase. We then enter into new relationships; we become friends, become citizens of a great society, become tender husbands, become fathers. Our duties expand with our circumstances, and our worries with our duties. All these manifold connections give our heart its share, call upon our understanding, and open a wide field of occupations to our strengths.

What sources of distress, of pain, of dissatisfaction! Our heart can deceive us; our understanding can err; our strengths are limited; success is not within our power. All our passions, all our desires, seem to revolt against our happiness <note>original: "Glückseligkeit" - a state of blessedness or supreme happiness</note>. We desire,

--- Page 205 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the internal conflict between human desires and moral duty. This page continues the exploration of the psychological torment caused by unfulfilled passions.</meta>

we wish, we languish. Should we quench our thirst? Repentance will follow! Should we overcome our desires? What self-denial <note>original: "Verläugnung"; refers to the difficult act of renouncing one's own impulses</note> is required?

An unsatisfied love, an unsatisfied anger gnaws at our heart, and drives all peace from the same. In vain does our reason tell us that we are thus sparing ourselves the torments <note>original: "Quaalen"</note> of an offended conscience. It is a poor comfort when we recognize that we could be even more unhappy than we are in fact. Why must these powerful passions <note>original: "Leidenschaften"</note> torment us, if we cannot satisfy them without the danger of being tormented even more in the future? The satisfaction of the desire for revenge <note>original: "Rachbegierde"</note> has something sweet, something pleasant, at least in the moment <note>original: "Augenblicke"</note> where we take our revenge. Should we not allow ourselves this single pleasant moment? Certainly, we foresee that we may repent it: but we are nonetheless unhappy as long as we have not satisfied it. A restrained desire for revenge turns the sharpness of its dagger upon ourselves, and lets us feel all that which we do not want to let our enemy feel.

<meta>This page continues the author's philosophical reflection on the internal struggles of the human soul and the inherent suffering found even in virtuous emotions.</meta>

And how often do we not have the opportunity to desire, without being permitted to receive; to love, without letting our love; to despise, without our contempt; to be angry, without letting this anger; to hate, without letting this hatred break out! All of them enemies of our peace, who are born in the midst of ourselves, in our own hearts! Unhappy life, when one has to fight almost constantly, or constantly to repent!

But it is not only our self-love <note>original: "Eigenliebe"; in this context, it refers to egoism or the instinct for self-preservation that can lead to selfishness</note>, not only these violent passions that rebel against others; even the benevolent inclinations, our philanthropy <note>original: "Menschenliebe"; literally "love of humanity"</note> itself, make us unhappy. The softer our heart is, the greater is our sensitivity <term>sensitivity: "Empfindlichkeit," a key 18th-century concept referring to a person's capacity for deep emotional response and sympathy</term>; the more universal our love for mankind is, the more we expose ourselves to the pains that wound our heart. We suffer through them in thousands. Or are the tears that a father weeps over his child, the husband over his beloved, the brother over the brother, the friend over the friend, the citizen over his fellow citizen, not true, not heartfelt tears?

What a wide prospect over scenes of misery, in which my compassion and my tender heart bid me take part, opens up here!

<meta>catchword: here!</meta>

<summary>The author reflects on the internal conflict between suppressed passions and the inherent sorrow that comes from empathy and the love of others.</summary>

Here! O Nature! O Nature! Why have you formed me tender, compassionate, and sensitive, if I was meant to be happy among the countless accidents of life! Everything drags me down in its fall! I answer sigh with sigh, tear with tear!

I had earned the hope, through care and toil, to enjoy the joys of a father: chance, or illness, or an excess <note>original: "Ausschweifung" – can refer to physical overindulgence or a moral lapse leading to ruin</note> tears my son—the darling of my heart, the hope of my old age—from my arms! I see him suffer, see him wrestling with death, and I cannot save him. My beloved, in whom I hoped to find my bliss <note>original: "Glückseligkeit" – a state of supreme happiness or beatitude</note>, allows herself to be seduced by a traitor, disgraces me, and presses a dagger into my heart. Or I myself am happy: but my beloved brother suffers. I see him in misery, feel all his pains, and forget my own favorable fate. Soon a malicious enemy persecutes me, and I suffer innocently; soon a friend dies in my arms, with whom I shared my heart and my fortune. I miss him; my tears demand him back; I can no longer enjoy my own prosperity <note>original: "Wohlstand" – here meaning general well-being and success</note>, nor all the pleasures which fate wishes to give me;

Take me back into your womb, dear Mother Nature! I was long a poor, unhappy orphan since men tore me from your arms; I did not run away from you willfully or faithlessly, and I always longed for you; but my fate had wedged me between insurmountable walls, and held me shackled to circumstances and time. ->The 10th of June.<-
<note>original: "Brachmonats" — an archaic German term for June, literally the "fallow month" in the agricultural calendar.</note>

I wish to flee the great masses of men and join myself to the small, quiet flock of the good and peaceful of this earth; that seems to me truly for the best. I was among them, and they played their games with me; I loved them, and they persecuted me. My friend says they are all worthless, and he would like to drown the greater part of them in the ocean <note>original: "Weltmeer"</note>; but that would only become a stinking puddle, which would surely poison the entire remainder of humanity with its foul vapors. My father meant better by them—the good man, he was likely deceived as well. — They may well have been better creatures <note>original: "Geschöpfe"</note> before they gathered together, built cities, and conspired toward their mutual downfall. — Yet I will not speak ill of them. I believe that, on the whole, they are not as bad as they appear here and there in individual cases; but they are certainly not good, that I have experienced—this follows from the state in which these willful refugees <note>original: "Flüchtlinge"</note> have placed themselves after abandoning Nature. Pride and self-interest <note>original: "Eigennutz"</note> make them dissatisfied with the Creator and with Nature; why should they be any less so with their own kind?

<meta>The previous page likely discussed the departure of humanity from a state of natural innocence; the catchword "Erfin" (from Erfindungen/Inventions) bridges to the critique of human ingenuity on this page.</meta>

They are ungrateful toward the Creator and Nature; why should they not be so toward one another? They have abandoned simplicity to distance themselves from the Truth; now their spirit stumbles about on wandering paths, upon which it has made all those beautiful discoveries and inventions that—sooner or later, but certainly one day—must consume the human race. Nature built man according to the climate and the products of the region where she had placed him, and separated him from his antipodes <note>Those living on the opposite side of the globe.</note> by immeasurable seas; but man embarked his life and his happiness into a hollow oak <note>A poetic reference to early wooden ships.</note> and swam to the far shore. Nature put no sword in his hand, and no fifty-pounder <note>A heavy artillery piece or cannonball.</note>; but he himself sharpened the steel for the murder of his brother, and shatters the lives of a hundred with a single crack. Nature hid the metals in the womb of the earth—intended perhaps for nobler purposes than those for which man uses them—and he snatched them from the earth with greedy rage. He invented poison, Greek fire <note>An incendiary weapon used in medieval warfare that continued to burn on water.</note>, and the art of printing—the means to spread universally everything that the spirit hatches in secret for the destruction of mankind, in order to make not thousands, but millions miserable. I wish to use these few moments to write down my thoughts, which have accumulated in my soul since I left the city. It shall not be a futile labor for me. --- Page 220 ---
Conscious hearts tremble and fear the end: but the terrors among men, the gruesome scenes that occur daily among them—whose heart should they not fill with anxiety? Who can be a witness to the most abominable human deeds without fear and horror throwing him to the ground?

Could I have ever thought it: even this earth, sown with lovely flowers, inhabited by joyful, singing, and leaping creatures, drinks in human blood. Oh no! Nevermore would I have thought that—nevermore thought to find the sacrifice of bloody hatred upon the holy grounds—consecrated to the rest of the blessed and to decay <note>original: "Verwesung"; refers to the natural process of decomposition in a cemetery</note>—murderer and murdered—a madman, upon whom his own despair avenges the blood of his brother. Oh, where shall I flee—where is there still a foot of land that men have not fertilized with their blood? Hatred, persecution, revenge, envy, and the blackest sins of every kind have likely already transformed the most blessed region, which nature formed into a blooming Eden, into a hell.

I entered the churchyard, crept with soft steps along the wall behind the graves until I was close to Simon’s tombstone, and positioned myself behind it, yet without being able to be noticed. I did not yet know him, the unfortunate man whose tears flowed upon Simon’s grave, whose heart became the prey of the fiercest grief and despair, whose frantic gestures seemed to betray the terrible sufferings that tortured his soul. Anxious, I awaited what would follow with trembling. It was as if I had only just seen his face—his wildly rolling eyes, from which dark, glowing flashes of lightning shot forth; his pale cheeks, furrowed by a constant stream of tears; the shattering of all human features; the violent twitching of every muscle—oh, what horror! For a while, he remained half-kneeling, his face pressed against the burial mound in silent pain, in brooding despair. Suddenly he pulled himself up again and let out a violent cry, torn from the innermost depths of his soul. I lost all consciousness from terror and cried out; he saw himself discovered, saw a witness to his pain, and—as he might believe—also a dangerous witness to the secret that was the source of his misery. He sprang up, caught sight of me, and now I recognized him.

He was Peter, the hunter. He was a faithful and industrious man in his service, and performed all his duties with the strictest precision; for this reason, his masters held him in especially high regard, and the whole village was fond of him, for he was thoroughly honest and straightforward. But his heart was hard, and his mind stubborn. His anger was highly dangerous, and glowed

in his heart, until his revenge ripened, and pleas and supplications could not reconcile him. I called out to him: Peter, what do you want? Do not disturb the peace of the blessed <note>The deceased Simon is referred to as "the blessed," a common 18th-century idiom for one who has passed away.</note> through your lamentations and your furious gestures; you do not do well at all to let yourself be carried away so far; for notice, the friend is no more, but your wife, your children still remain, and all your fellow men who still live justly call you to account for every minute of your life that you rob from them through the excess of your grief; your days are numbered for the service of your fellow men, and you squander them. You are a husband, a father, a citizen, a human being — worthy names! — dear duties that demand your preservation."

"Come, dear Peter! I will lead you home to your wife and children; rest, and leave it to time to heal your pain. Come!" —— He fixed his gaze upon the earth; then, after a pause: "Dear Sir! You may well mean well by me; but things can never be well with me again. Look! — Here is my world; — there, right next to Simon's, is my house. I can perform no more important service for the world than to die. —— My wife — my children —— ah! Simon also had a wife and children, and I — — —" A terrible pause. The face of this unfortunate man was the most horrific thing one could behold; the expression of his grief in mien and gesture outweighed in horror all cries of lamentation. I had to gather all my strength and summon all my courage to banish the fear from my heart that terrified me more and more from time to time. I ventured once more; I took him by the hand and tried to pull him with me; with wild turbulence <note>original: "Ungeſtimm," an archaic term for violent agitation or vehemence</note> he tore himself loose and cried: "Leave me! Let me die here! I should have done so long ago, and yet I still live."

Murderer! — You? — Poor man! You are insane. — —

Now, listen to it once more: I am a murderer — — Simon’s murderer. — Do you hear! What a terrible, hollow sound that word makes! — I still hear it distinctly in the air — how it rushes along, from evening until morning, and is repeated countless millions of times. All beings in nature tremble, hearing: Murderer! repeating: Murderer! and crying out: Curse! — Curse upon the murderer! — —

Sit down with me! There, across from Simon’s grave, so that we may keep it in our sight, and I will tell you the terrible story. Do not stare at me so, do not doubt: It is I. Have no pity for me; it would be a sin if you were to deceive your heart on my account; pity belongs to the unfortunate — — Curse upon the villain! — But listen! — The hour of midnight has passed — the bell strikes one — and it must never be day again. — I am in haste, so that the sun does not shine upon my death. Such a magnificent funeral candle <note>original: "Sterbkerze" — literally a "death candle," referring here to the sun as a light for his passing</note> is not for a wretch such as I.

# Page 213

Simon and I had already been good friends for many years, and he showed me many kindnesses. He advanced me money in urgent cases, as much as he could spare, and never pressed me for payment. We were <term>Gevattern</term> <note>Godparents; in this context, it implies a deep spiritual and familial bond between the two men</note>, and what he did for my children— — God! In him, I have also murdered their father, for I could never truly be one to them. In the evening we liked to sit together—he never went to the tavern in all his days—we ate supper together, and then we usually played cards or games, with the exception of the eves of holidays and feast days, which Simon preferred to spend with a more worthy occupation. 

Gaming was always my primary passion. In my life, it had already cost me a good sum of money and often brought me into great misfortune; but I did not let myself be warned by this, and I yielded to this wretched passion for so long until it finally plunged me into the most terrible abyss. Simon was never a gambler in his life, but I enticed him into playing with me. He did that, too, more out of kindness and love for me—to keep me from playing in the taverns and elsewhere—rather than for his own pleasure. Since we did not play for large sums of money

I surely had a premonition that I was signing Simon’s and my own death warrant. His friendly reproaches and faithful exhortations that I should manage my household more wisely embittered me even more against him, and revenge — revenge boiled in my breast — revenge my heart had sworn.

->The 13th of June.<- <note>original: "Brachmonats" — an archaic German term for June, the "fallow month."</note>

What bliss it is to find a soul that stands as an exception among the common swarm of humanity! — How gladly I wish to forget the pains caused to me by the frequent examples of faithlessness, deceit, and cruelty of men. Oh, this day shall be a festive one for me as long as I live. It soothed my outraged heart and poured joy into my soul, which had long sighed under the pressure of terrible sufferings. Whatever sadness — whatever misfortune may strike me this day, I will not disturb its celebration with lamentations, and no tear shall dim my eye! — — Blessed fields! inhabited by such noble creatures — happy country folk — in whose bosom such a good soul dwells — in the lowly, moss-covered hut I wish to forget the world; my world is only here. ->The 14th of June.<- <note>original: "Brachmonat," an archaic German term for June, literally the "fallow month."</note>

It was the most beautiful summer morning that ever woke the earth from gentle sleep, and joy laughed toward all creatures upon their awakening, as I left my bed and quickly fled from the house, so as not to lose a single one of the blessed moments that I enjoy on a beautiful morning.

Dear children! You make my old age so happy; you make life so dear to me that I cannot resist the futile wish that I might only now begin to live, so that I could grow old with you all over again. Become a brave man—strong and noble—and may God pour his blessing upon everything you sow and plant, and let you enjoy the fruits of your industry even in your gray old age. May your father one day feel the same joy in his grandchildren that you give me today, dear children! And may the happiness of my old age become your lot. — — — Be industrious, child! Faithful and upright <note>original: "bieder" - a term implying integrity, honesty, and plain-dealing</note>, as amiable as a man as you are now as a boy; then God will rejoice in you; you will be happy and live to see my years.

The little one fell around his neck and showered him with kisses; Lisgen embraced his knees and drew one of his hands toward her; the father and mother of the good children embraced the old man and bathed him with their tears. Bliss overwhelmed all their hearts and senses.

Children! Children! I am overcome! was all that the happy old man, with a trembling voice, could—

--- Page 251 ---
is also as indifferent to my heart as the death of any other unknown person in the world; and yet I am expected to mourn for her—to weep for her death; but no! that is not actually demanded: I am merely supposed to dress in black for a few days, in order to appear to be what I am not. I wonder if the shade of my female cousin would be angry if I wore a green coat on the day of her funeral? — — But thus everything is deception. The eye weeps, and the heart laughs; and one wears a black coat today because fashion does not permit a green one.

Here they bury the dead with mournful singing, and there with tones of jubilation and dancing upon their graves; in one place they let them rot in the earth, elsewhere they burn them; the Egyptians snatched them away from decay entirely and made mummies out of them—a custom that would have done honor to the human heart if it had been based on gratitude and respect for the noble; but even there, the pride of the rich and powerful maintained its <term>monopoly</term> <note>exclusive control or possession</note> over it. The Romans hired mourning women <note>original: "Klageweiber" — professional mourners common in ancient Mediterranean cultures</note> who wept for the deceased for money, and we — — — — — The Hindustani widow allows herself to be burned with her husband because the etiquette of her gender demands that she should not outlive him, and our women quickly dry the artificial tear from their eye as soon as the funeral procession has passed, pouring their grief into the bosom of their lover, who becomes their second husband after the weeks of mourning.

In this way, people play their games with one another, and deceive the one who believes them in death, just as they deceived him during his life.

I have often heard it said that death makes us all equal: but the proverb lies. Just look at that great marble monument, upon which art and wealth have been squandered to excess. Does it honor the memory of a nobleman? Of a brave citizen who died rich in deeds for humanity? — No! It is the tomb of the wealthy Clinias <note>Likely a reference to a figure from Greek antiquity, used here as a type for the idle rich</note>. Clinias occupies the greatest space for himself even here, and pushes his poorer brothers away from him, just as he drew the share of hundreds to himself during his life. Clinias has also forestalled the forgetfulness of men. He knew well that their ingratitude no longer remembers the noble and the good who were among them once the earth covers them; all the more would they forget an unworthy man who did nothing at all for humanity. But he has established an anniversary <note>German: "Jahrtag" - a foundation or endowment for a yearly memorial service</note> for himself, in order to awaken annually in the hearts of his fellow citizens the thought:

### July 1st. <note>original: "Heumonats" — literally "Hay month," an archaic German term for July.</note>

I have achieved my purpose, and I can rejoice in the little that I received, without my heart reproaching me for having made anyone else unhappy through my actions. I have only a third of the whole, and I am happy; do I need more to fulfill my few desires? — Perhaps my female relative <note>original: "Anverwandte"</note> is not as satisfied or as happy with two-thirds as I am. Perhaps her heart is insatiable, nourishes more and more violent desires, and knows more and more pressing needs. Oh, may she always be as happy with the greater portion as I am with the lesser.

Do you wish to go into the city?

# On the Degradation of Art and Nature

It is a reckless audacity to surrender one's life to an art that is of no use to all of humanity; an art which, at most, serves only to show how deeply man has been degraded—and how far man has progressed in the art of working against nature. <note>The author is likely referring to acrobats or "tightrope walkers" who performed dangerous stunts for entertainment.</note>

Above all, a young girl, who could not yet have reached a full twelve years of age, drew my attention to herself. "She may yet become quite skillful in time," someone beside me remarked. "Perhaps," a bystander replied drily, "if she does not break her neck before that time." In a state of total anxiety, I quickly looked around for the girl to see if she were still alive, and my heart trembled for her life. I was told she was an orphan whom the troupe <note>original: "Gesellschaft"</note> had taken in while traveling. Without parents, without help, and likely without having learned anything by which she could support herself, the director <term>Principal: the head of a traveling theater or circus troupe</term> took her in. Her name and her further fate I do not know; but—God!—I know enough—I know that she is unhappy, and will become increasingly so.

# On the Destiny of Man

Consider the destiny of man; ha! what is the destiny of man? What value does he possess? — All too often, his destiny is that of a beast of burden, and his value is even less than that.

But how? Is it possible? Could the Showman-King <note>original: "Gauckler-König"; likely referring to a traveling circus master or troupe leader who exercised absolute authority over his performers</note> count the poor girl among his slaves without first receiving consent from the State in which she was unfortunately born? Or must one have parents and friends, be wealthy and of high birth, in order to belong to the State? — Should the State not turn red with shame over its carelessness, wake from its sleep, and see that hundreds of its children are miserable? Should the State not say: No! We will not abandon any of our children to such a wretched fate; we wish to assign the girl a nobler destiny, a sphere of activity more worthy of a human being; we want to train her to be a wife, a mother, a good housewife; we want to provide her with a dowry, and she shall bring happiness to one of our brave citizens. How beautiful, how dignified such an action would be! — and yet, it is nothing more than a duty. I would gladly grant the vanity of a Fatherland that could point to such a noble fulfillment of its maternal duties

<summary>The author critiques the State's neglect of marginalized individuals, specifically a young girl enslaved by a showman, arguing that the government has a moral duty to provide for the welfare and social integration of all its citizens.</summary>
<keywords>human destiny, social welfare, state responsibility, slavery, women's roles, citizenship, poverty</keywords>

->The 6th of July<- <note>original: "Heumonat," literally "hay month," an archaic German term for July.</note>

Tell me, where is the land of the good? — Should I sail toward the North or the South; shall I find it in the Orient <note>original: "Morgenland," literally "morning land."</note>, or toward the West? — Oh, I would so gladly go there, to be among them! — I do not fear rocky Arabia, nor the sandy deserts of Libya; I will climb over the Carpathian Mountains and swim through the ocean — only let me find the fatherland of the good. — Or do they have no portion on this earth? Are they strangers here below, and is their mother-soil in one of the immeasurable suns in the high starry sky? — — Or is it also a chimera, like Utopia and Plato’s Republic? — That is most likely the case.

Good people are found everywhere — but so rarely, so hidden, that many of them die unnoticed; years — centuries roll over them, and their memory lies irretrievably buried in the rubble of time.

<summary>The author expresses a deep longing to find a society of truly virtuous people, questioning if such a place exists on Earth or if it is merely a philosophical ideal like Utopia. Early on, he already formed himself for the service of humanity and his fatherland, and early on he also succeeded in being employed in public affairs. With unparalleled courage and perseverance, he applied the strength of his youth to the welfare of the state. as his body gained new strength and ever more stability, his zeal to serve also increased, as did his courage to defy the dangers of his profession and to boldly stand against the malice of men.

Now he is an old man <note>original: "Greis"</note> of more than seventy years, and he enjoys the bliss of a venerable old age, which he has reached in the service of humanity and his fatherland.

On the final days of his life, he looks back joyfully upon those past, and in the long sequence of many years, there is none of which he should be ashamed. His highest wish was always to be of use, and all his efforts were directed toward achieving this great desire; it pained him deeply when insurmountable obstacles hindered him or thwarted his endeavors.

To still have the joy in his final days of knowing a good person was made happy through him—one who would follow him on the path he had trodden—was the only happiness he still desired for himself. Death had long ago relieved him of his paternal duties; two promising sons died in the bloom of youth; and his heart now belonged undivided to humanity.

<meta>The catchword "Er" (He) from the previous page introduces the subject of the narrative: a man of high character facing hardship.</meta>

A man, whose abilities as well as the dignity of his **moral character** <note>original: "moralischer Charakter"; referring to the integrity and ethical standing of the individual</note> had long entitled him to an **office** <note>original: "Amt"; a formal administrative or governmental position</note>, was nevertheless forced to languish for a long time without bread and in the utmost poverty alongside his wife and children. He left nothing undone in his efforts to earn a living. He sought friends, but found none; he applied whenever a position became vacant, but he was always passed over. The noble old man knew him—knew his merits and his sad fate. To help the unfortunate man became his sole intention. He thought of making him his associate and leaving him behind as the successor to his office after his death. Meanwhile, someone was found who offered him two thousand **guilders** <note>original: "Gulden"; a common currency in German-speaking lands during this period</note> in cash, along with the lifelong enjoyment of his position, if he would resign it in exchange for this sum. A tempting proposal!—Anyone whose failings are self-interest and a love of comfort would certainly not hesitate for long. One could conclude one's days so quite comfortably in a life of luxury and procure many pleasures for oneself.

But the noble old man thought more worthily; money had no value in his eyes, and **doing good** <note>original: "Wohlthun"</note> he always preferred to **good living** <note>original: "Wohlleben"; a life of ease or luxury</note>.

What might be the cause of this? — Is it the sheer quantity of pleasures? Is there a lack of variety? Are they not tasteful, not new, not charming enough? Or is pleasure itself not pleasurable enough to fulfill the desires of the city-dweller? — Does it lack truth, or greatness? Does it merely intoxicate, rather than refresh and strengthen? — Does it leave behind a void in the heart that no pleasure of this kind can fill? Or is it perhaps even accompanied by pain, dragging vexation and remorse in its wake? Perhaps the secret fault often lies within the heart of the discontented man <note>original: "Unvergnügten"</note>, such that he can never truly be well? — —

I posed all these questions to myself yesterday, as I left a public circle of joy late at night in the company of Mr. N. and his wife. It was a ball, and Mr. N. forced me with all his might to go along. I was glad from the bottom of my heart when I returned to my room, where I could be quiet and left to myself. Masquerades do grant one essential advantage: that the wicked, without fear—though one might wish to read the brand of their hearts upon their foreheads—may mingle among honest people. For the sake of propriety, the ladies need not blush at the ambiguous wit <note>The author refers to "double entendres" or suggestive jokes common in 18th-century social gatherings.</note> of the gentlemen and can safely smile their approval from behind the mask. Masquerades also have this good quality: they often present a person in their proper station, from which stubborn fortune had displaced them into another that suits them least of all. I saw charlatans <term>Charlatans: traveling quacks or street performers claiming medical skill</term> with so much naturalness and peculiarity of character that I would have bet a doctor was hidden behind the mask. A few village girls appeared with all the clumsy, peasant-like manner and the coarseness of their degraded class; but I was told they were women of high standing—I would never have believed that in all my days.

I would be willing to let that pass, however, if people only appeared as something other than they are while at the ball; but many go through their entire lives without ever leaving the masquerade.

I know that I will not find the joys of the countryside, for which my heart so fervently longed while in the city, until I can share them with Louise; I pass by the beauties of nature coldly until I can show them to her. Lonely and sad, I shall leave my business in the evening and sit in a dark thicket, thinking of Louise.

But only a few days more—and I shall come to life again, for I shall live with her, and enjoy with her the bliss of life. — —

Since that day, most of the leaves in Aglais’s <term>diary</term> <note>original: "Tagebuch"</note> were partly torn out; the rest were entirely blank, or contained only a fragment here and there. ->Aglais<-

<vocab>Louise, Aglais, diary, history, fragments, dates</vocab>

<summary>The narrator expresses a deep, melancholic longing for Louise, finding no joy in nature without her presence, followed by an editorial note explaining that Aglais's diary becomes fragmented from this point forward.</summary>

--- Page 299 ---
Aglais departed the following morning for his destination, with longing and love in his heart, and a pining hope for Louise. He was inseparably attached to her, and his heart willingly followed the impressions that the girl's advantageous education <note>The German "Bildung" here refers to both her physical form and her refined upbringing</note> had made upon his senses. Rhapsodizing, and with a soul susceptible to the highest ideal love, he lay in the sweet delusion that Louise would—must—love just as fervently and nobly as he, and that her heart must overflow with the same tender sentiments as his own.

Meanwhile, Louise remained merely one of the ordinary daughters of the city. She promised herself to Aglais because he was young, handsome, and especially because he held a professional post <note>original: "bedienstet"; implying he had a secure civil or military position</note>. She would have been just as inclined to give her hand to any other man with these, or even fewer advantages, as she was to give it to the good youth. Her heart was never consulted in the matter, or rather, through her upbringing and the ways of the world, it was already accustomed to saying "yes" hypocritically to everything. She knew love only through the depictions in romance novels and the gallantries of young gentlemen who had been courting her <note>original: "la cour gemacht"; from the French "faire la cour"</note> since her twelfth year. Her parents, following the usual patterns, thought no better of it. Provided that their daughter sewed and embroidered prettily, spoke French, and played the

That she played the <term>clavier</term> <note>An early keyboard instrument, likely a harpsichord or fortepiano</note>, and otherwise possessed an irreproachable reputation, and would now make an advantageous match, was all that satisfied them regarding their child's happiness.

Louise did not understand the language of the heart that flowed from Aglais's lips; what she returned to him was vain, affected novel-chatter and phrases from comedies. Aglais, enchanted by his girl, mistook it for the language of love, for the outpourings of the heart—taking the lie for the truth.

Louise’s character became even more corrupted by her mother, who, through both example and words, instilled in her the basest principles regarding her future husband. Aglais received this letter one evening, just as he was leaving his work; his heart was oppressed by hope and anxious forebodings, and being full of Louise's image, he intended to set out on his favorite walk along the banks of a river.

With a hand trembling with joy, he broke the seal, imagining for once that he would read happy news. In his usual manner, the entire letter was taken in with a single glance; he turned pale, his knees shook, he doubted, and looked once more—and two or three times at the superscription <note>The address written on the outside of the letter</note>, reading it letter by letter, and it was always addressed to Aglais; he rubbed his eyes perhaps a hundred times and began the letter again from the start, but the cruel script remained exactly as the hand of the base man <note>original: "Niederträchtigen," implying someone of low character or malicious intent</note> had stained it there.

<meta>The previous page likely detailed the moment Aglais received the devastating news or letter that led to his collapse by the river.</meta>

A leap into the rushing stream would likely have been the only thought a fiery, noble, and loving youth could still grasp in such a situation; but Aglais sank unconscious to the earth. The schoolmaster from his village happened to be walking this way; he found him lying there without signs of life and was terrified for the good youth, whom he—like everyone else in the village—held in the depths of his heart. Since he found no sign of a violent death upon him and still felt a faint pulse, he realized that it must only be a sudden fainting spell, and he brought him back home as best he could over the short distance. Through the help of the barber-surgeon <note>original: "Bader" — a historical medical practitioner who performed minor surgery, bloodletting, and dental work</note>, who was quickly summoned, Aglais was finally brought back to himself after many vain attempts. He looked around wildly, marveled at the crowd surrounding him, and in that moment of supreme agony, his bleeding heart felt little gratitude for human help, resentful that he had been torn back from the very gates of death. Meanwhile, the curiosity of the bystanders had taken possession of the letter that Aglais had held in his hands—which the schoolmaster, in his initial flurry of terror, had not noticed—and the secret of his love lay bare before everyone's eyes.

On the second day, Aglais found himself somewhat better; his sensory faculties returned; but only now did his soul feel the pain in its full extent. He could suffer no one around him; left alone to himself and his raging grief, he locked himself in his room, lamented, wept, and bored the inflicted wound ever deeper. He imagined Louise as the poor, innocent victim of the self-interest <note>original: "Eigennuzes"</note> and cruelty of her parents, and believed her heart would suffer just as much, and just as violently, as his own.

He continued to read through the shameful letter, and with shuddering and horror he now remembered that only a day and a half remained between him and Louise—between him and his life. That very evening, he ordered the best horse that could be found in the village and galloped with it through the entire night toward the city. He arrived in the morning, and in fourteen hours he had covered a distance of more than twelve miles <note>A German mile at this time was roughly 4.6 English miles, meaning he traveled over 55 miles in a single night.</note>. "O my Louise!" — — was all that Aglaïs could bring forth as he rushed toward her. Aglais had already been absent for five days from the place where he administered the office of **Tax Collector** <note>German: "Einnehmer" – a local official responsible for collecting taxes or revenues</note>, and on the second day after his departure, a report was already submitted to the government regarding his "secret flight," as they called it. A **Commission** <note>An official investigative body</note> was established to examine the state of affairs as he had left them.

For seven weeks Aglais lay ill; for seven weeks Death wrestled for a life that Aglais himself would have willingly surrendered, had not a more powerful art <note>Referring to the medical skill of his doctors</note> torn him from Death's hands. But during this time, the malice of men and of fate worked toward his complete ruin.

<meta>The previous page likely detailed the onset of Aglais's illness and the legal or professional machinations against him. The catchword "von" (of/from) connects to the description of his mental state.</meta>

The strictest precision prevailed. The unfortunate man could prevent nothing of all that transpired; he could not justify himself, for he did not even know how shamefully he was being treated; he was ill, and for the most part, out of his mind. His office <note>referring to his professional position or civil service post</note> was taken from him; the largest part of his fortune, which he had been required to deposit as security <note>original: "Caution"; a mandatory financial bond for officials</note>, became the prey of the most shameful villainy <note>original: "Bubenstücks"</note>, and they did not even grant him a pension. Aglais recovered his health and received this news with such a senseless indifference that it astonished everyone who knew him, yet moved few souls to compassion. The excess of grief, the supreme suffering he had already endured, had made him blunt to further feeling; and the illness had too greatly weakened his senses and exhausted his body for this terrible news to affect him with its full power. The body still lived a weak, animal life; but the spirit was dead, and for some time Aglais resembled an automaton <note>original: "Automat"; a mechanical figure acting without conscious thought</note> more than a human being.

For another quarter of a year, the shadow of Aglais—for it was no longer he himself—wandered among men; but he fled from them as the fearful traveler flees the deadly serpent; he avoided their sight like the poisonous gaze of the basilisk <note>In folklore, a basilisk is a mythical reptile said to kill with a single glance.</note>, and their voices were more painful to his ear than the croaking of ravens and more terrible than the howling of wolves. ->The 20th of the Wind-month.<- <note>The "Wind-month" (Windmonat) is an archaic German name for November.</note>

I have noticed that often one who preaches the truth does not wish to hear it from anyone else, because out of pride he regards himself as the sole possessor of it; or—yes! I have experienced it—he himself does not even know the Divine truth.

Be not too hasty in your veneration for this man; he preaches virtue to you as the wolf preaches gentleness and the fox preaches honesty; but he is more predatory than a wolf and more cunning than a fox. He only wishes to extract profit for his own self-interest from virtue.

Look! He carries the torch of Enlightenment <term>Aufklärung: the 18th-century intellectual movement emphasizing reason and individualism</term> before him—at times it also hands you the steaming pitch—he spreads light over the globe, and you see—that the wolf wears sheep's clothing, and the fox creeps in the hypocritical robes of a penitent, that the tiger warns you with a false, smiling friendliness against the grim expression of the lion;—but your ruin is nonetheless certain because of it;—you only see so that you may see how unhappy man is in the world;—everything is light, and

->The 30th of November.<-
<note>original: "Windmonats" — an archaic German term for the "windy month," or November.</note>

Strange! An acquaintance of mine gave me the list today of those unfortunate souls who were executed under his official administration. They were poor day-laborers <term>Taglöhner: unskilled workers paid by the day, often living in extreme poverty</term>, who were left with only the single choice between hunger and the executioner's death; impoverished peasants who were forced to surrender their property and possessions to the —— ——. Hated orphans of fortune, whom the pressures of life had degraded into beasts of prey; and here and there a villain who, once across the border, is an honest man again. But not one wealthy, respected, or titled man: no "Monochrome" and no "Butterfly." <note>The author likely uses "Einfärbigter" (Monochrome) and "Papillion" (Butterfly) as metaphors for stiff bureaucrats and flighty, fashionable aristocrats.</note> Beyond the Channel <note>referring to England</note>, I know that they hang His Lordship on the same gallows as the highwayman <note>original: "Hygwaymann"</note>. Different countries, different customs! ——

### The 18th of December.
<note>original: "Christmonat" — an archaic German term for December, literally "Christ-month."</note>

The female sex is highly unreasonable in its demands. When a woman is unfaithful to her husband and flutters from one lover to another, she still remains jealous of both husband and lover. They demand fidelity, yet maintain none themselves.

"Listen, friend!" F*** said to me today, "I am unhappy, most unhappy. Pity me, comfort me; give me counsel!" — "What has happened to you?" "Oh, I loved a girl—loved her beyond words. She was poor, but I believed she was good. The richest and most distinguished beauties of the city made proposals to me, and I rejected them. But for my honesty I was betrayed, and for my love I was made into the most wretched of men. My wife is beautiful, charming, young; I found virtue <note>original: "Tugend" — a central Enlightenment concept referring to moral excellence and modesty.</note> in her—or at least I imagined I found it in her; but she is a true fury. ->The 22nd of December.<-
<note>original: "Christmonat" — an archaic German term for December, literally "Christ-month."</note>

I cannot believe that there are people who possess the secret art of calling the souls of the deceased back to Earth. Man's only consolation—to finally find peace beyond the reach of men—would be lost; all hope of ever escaping their persecutions would be gone. They would not leave the honest souls of good people, who in life were the playthings of their evil passions, unmolested even in the realm of rest; they would fetch them over from eternity, and—God knows!—what they would make of them.

Waiting upon them, lying at their feet, carrying their trains, and—above all—extorting money—that would certainly be the task assigned to the souls of men. I have often entertained myself with this idea. A wretched pedant <note>original: "Schulfux" — a derogatory term for a narrow-minded schoolmaster or "pedant."</note> would summon Virgil and Horace, and tear old Homer from the lap of the Muses, just to read him his commentary, which Homer would not even understand. Hortensius and Cicero <note>Famous Roman orators known for their eloquence in law and politics.</note> would become the unfortunate victims of the lawyers, and Socrates — — — — God preserve us from such a science!

<summary>The author reflects skeptically on necromancy, arguing that if the living could summon the dead, they would only use the opportunity to further exploit and harass virtuous souls and classical figures.</summary>
<keywords>necromancy, afterlife, skepticism, Virgil, Horace, Homer, Cicero, Socrates, December</keywords>

<meta>The previous page discussed the transmigration of souls and the reversal of social hierarchies in the afterlife. The catchword "am" suggests a continuation of the thought regarding the nature of the soul.</meta>

But viewed from another perspective, it would also be quite humorous. Mulei Ismael <note>Moulay Ismaïl (1645–1727), a powerful and notoriously cruel Sultan of Morocco</note> and Christian <note>original: "Christier"; likely referring to King Christian II of Denmark, known as a tyrant</note> would now become the slaves of one of their subjects, whose ancestors once had to work themselves to death for them. And—one could thoroughly study the "physiognomy of souls" <note>A play on physiognomy, the pseudo-scientific study of determining character from facial features; here applied to the literal appearance of the soul</note>. How many a charming beauty would then present a hideous figure, and the soul of a powerful "fat-belly" <note>original: "Dickwanstes"; a derogatory term for a wealthy, gluttonous man</note> would rattle with thinness like a skeleton. And the moral philosophers, I think, would write a different system of ethics <note>original: "Etik"</note> if they could see the diseased souls. There are truly sick souls among us. Some have consumption <note>tuberculosis</note>, very many have vertigo, leprosy, and incurable infirmity; many have the sniffles, and a great many are lame, hunchbacked, squinting—or even blind. — — —

<meta>This page continues the philosophical reflections and biographical sketches found in the preceding sections, focusing on the nature of deception and the character of Aglais.</meta>

Why is deception such a universal vice among mankind? — I believe I can easily answer this question for myself. Our senses deceive us; head and heart delude us; the weakness of our powers, vanity, and a lack of insight cause us to willingly deceive ourselves, and all too often we do not feel the deception, and even find pleasure in it, even when we know that it is an illusion. Why then should one person not deceive another? —

This is now everything that could be gathered from the life of Aglais. These are fragments of *enthusiasm* <note>original: "Schwärmerey" – a term often used in the 18th century to describe excessive emotionalism, religious fanaticism, or visionary dreaming</note>. Aglais’s heated imagination saw the world from a completely different point of view than it truly is. One sees from everything how much a human being depends on circumstances; at one moment his style of writing is tuned to a gentle low, then elevated to the visionary, then indignant, and filled with expressions that I sometimes wished had no place in his writing tablet; however, I did not want to change anything. For those who think and know the human heart, these fragments will provide material for reflection;

## E s s a y s.

Holy are the quiet joys of nature. Holy are the hours of love. My thanks to you, female friend! <note>The original German "Freundinn" specifically denotes a female friend, likely referring to a close confidante or romantic partner.</note> for the bliss you granted me. Here I wish to record it, that hour, as one of the most blessed of my life—the hour of a spring evening. The moon shone in the sky, the veil of night softly covered the landscape, as I, intoxicated with joy, walked on the arm of the best of women.

Oh! I could live so entirely for a creature who also lived entirely for me.

I would allow myself to be locked in a nutshell with the beloved of my soul, and imagine myself to be a king. <note>This is a literary allusion to Shakespeare's *Hamlet*: "I could be bounded in a nutshell and count myself a king of infinite space."</note>

But where is the woman who truly feels? Her finery is everything to her trifling heart, coquetry fills every gap; where is there still a place for the soul's feeling? <term>Seelengefühl: a Romantic-era term describing a deep, spiritual capacity for emotion and empathy.</term>

The rose lasts for only a month, the
thistle lasts longer; I would rather be a rose.

I search always and find nothing; my heart is so crowded, I feel lonely among people, and find peace only when I am entirely alone, for then it is as if something invisible were around me, drying my tears and whispering into the ear of my soul: it will be better there <note>The author likely refers to the afterlife or a spiritual "beyond."</note>.

When I die, this shall be my consolation: that I did good to many people who repaid me with evil. —

I have many debts owed to me, but I never demand payment; if only God rewards my friends for it, for I am an honest man and can leave them nothing else.

When I come to die, and should I become sad, I will think to myself that there are lawyers and theologians, advocates and judges in the world, and I will be glad that I am dying <note>A satirical jab at the professional classes of the 18th/19th century, suggesting the afterlife is preferable to a world full of litigation and dogma.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the author's philosophical and spiritual meditation on the nature of divine and platonic love, following the previous page's introduction of the soul's higher aspirations.</meta>

...the enjoyment of an immortal soul; a warming, kindling, pure passion—the passion of the angels, which ignites them toward their kindred spirits!

Lustful thoughts and bold desires do not mingle within your holy flame; you strive nobler and higher, lightening the breast of thoughts that press it down toward the earth, and gladdening it with heavenly bliss.

The gods <note>The author uses the plural "Götter" here, likely a poetic or classical reference common in 18th-century literature, despite the monotheistic context that follows.</note> gave you, oh heavenly gift, to mankind, in order to take all the burden of earthly woe from them, and to let them taste the joys of heaven.

God! You who are love itself, release my tongue from the shackles of prejudice, and let me proclaim to my brothers that you are love.

They shall not say of me that my tongue stammers when I speak of Him who is love.

I do not wish to sit upon the thresholds of the temple which the hands of mortals built for the Infinite One: in the flowery meadow, in the lonely valley, I will proclaim Him for whom the flowers breathe their scents, and to whom the larks sing praises in the morning.

<note>The author emphasizes a "Natural Theology," suggesting that God is better worshipped in nature—the "lonely valley"—than in man-made structures.</note>

<meta>The catchword "mir" (to me) from the previous page connects to the opening sentence of this page. The author continues a pantheistic meditation on finding the divine through nature.</meta>

There I will venture into the sanctuary <term>Heiligthum: a holy place or inner sanctum</term> of nature, listen to the voice that my heart speaks, and give ear to the premonition <term>Ahndung: an archaic form of 'Ahnung,' referring to a spiritual presentiment or intuition</term> that streams from heaven with the dew, which refreshes the roses in the thicket and the lilies in the field.

From the noblest images of nature I will borrow the colors to paint Him whom my heart worships, and my brush shall be more glorious than the brush of a cherub <note>original: "Cherubs"</note>, who first painted the Godhead <note>original: "Gottheit"</note> in the sun for the weak eye of the mortal. —

Rapture carries me away—where am I! Furthermore, a portrait was found of a noble, gentle lady <note>original: "Frauenzimmer," a period-appropriate term for a woman of status</note>; it was inscribed as follows:

> You alone, gentle creature, were Aglaia’s <note>Aglaia is one of the three Graces in Greek mythology, representing splendor and beauty</note> friend; you were, and are no more; in the hereafter I shall find you again.

Beside this portrait, the following was written on torn-off sheets of paper:

> My God! You have placed me in this world too soon; perhaps posterity will recognize You better, and walk more nobly before Your eyes.

> O Creator of worlds! Watch over those who deform <note>original: "verstalten," meaning to disfigure or mar</note> the holy work of Your hands.

> Call the mothers back to their first duties, and the fathers to the actions of the first men <note>A reference to a perceived lost state of primordial or "natural" virtue</note>.

--- Page 340 ---
<meta>The catchword "God" from the previous page connects to the opening reflection on the Creator's unchanging nature despite human fickleness.</meta>

That which men once regarded as the blessing of heaven, they now look upon as a curse; and yet You, Infinite One, have not changed their joys.

How foolish the children of men are; they cling so gladly to the miraculous, and fail to recognize the wonders of the Father of Nature. They prize the fragile works of their own hands higher than the immortal masterpieces of a God, from whose master hand came Nature and the architecture of the worlds. They praise the childish, decaying handiwork of their own hands, and look with indifference upon the whole of creation; they marvel at houses of cards, which a breath may topple, and look unmoved as millions of suns revolve in their orbits according to eternal laws.

They linger over artificial scenes and deceptive paintings, and abandon the sublime spectacle that Nature offers them: they withdraw their eyes from the great effects of the celestial bodies. I saw them, the fools, leave the sun to gather by the pale light of their torches; I saw them close their spoiled ears to the artless songs of the birds, to be enchanted by the disharmonious noise of their blustering instruments.

<note>The author uses "blustering instruments" (rauschenden Instrumente) likely to contrast the organic, divine music of the natural world with the mechanical, loud social entertainments of the 18th-century urban elite.</note>

<summary>A philosophical reflection on human vanity and the tendency to value artificial, man-made achievements over the enduring and sublime wonders of the natural world and the cosmos.</summary>
<keywords>Nature, Creation, Divine Architecture, Astronomy, Human Folly, Materialism vs. Spirituality</keywords>

--- Page 341 ---
God of Nature! You gave to man smiling meadows and refreshing shade, and they built themselves thick walls, locking themselves into narrow prisons which they call cities. There they heap themselves up like a herd of slaves; one oppresses the other and blocks his free breath, instead of spreading themselves across God’s earth without being harmful to one another. They gather only upon a few points of the globe, and the greatest part of the remaining surface they leave uninhabited and uncultivated.

Beneficent Father of Beings! You directed man to the bubbling spring, gave him the nourishing milk of the herds, the sweet honey of the industrious bee; and he is not satisfied with all of that; he invented for himself intoxicating and deadly poison <note>likely referring to distilled spirits or strong alcohol</note>, which he drinks in small draughts from golden vessels. You let countless fruits flourish for his nourishment; but the most wholesome herbs, everything nutritious and strengthening that the plant kingdom <term>Pflanzenreich: the botanical world</term> possesses, does not still his hunger; like the ravening wolf he strangles, and blood must gush forth from between his teeth. The monsters! <note>original: "Unmenschen" – literally "un-humans" or those who have lost their humanity</note> Their tiger-hearts do not tremble,

<summary>A passionate lament addressed to the Creator, contrasting the natural gifts of the earth with the artificial, crowded, and violent lifestyle of modern urban civilization. --- Page 342 ---
when they slaughter the young lamb before the eyes of its mother. The ungrateful ones! They lead the hardworking ox home, still dripping with sweat from the plow, and he falls beneath their murderous axe! Those whose fields he has only just fertilized now feast upon his still-quivering flesh.

Lord! Tell me, where is there yet a corner of the earth where one can live according to nature? Show me a desolate island, a barren, arid rock, where man, peaceful and secure, could worship your eternal justice! Ha! Look! The bloody demon of war and the spirit of despotism have divided the world between them. Everywhere one encounters traces of them, and they reach out their hands to one another from one end of the world to the other.

We have come a long way. Our women are proud of their infertility and consider their vanity an honor. They are sensitive <note>In this context, "sensitive" implies a refined or artificial aversion rather than genuine feeling.</note> toward the innocent caresses of a newborn. People always want to pluck the flowers from Cupid's <note>original: "Amor," the Roman god of desire and erotic love.</note> month of roses, but they do not wish to wait for the fruits of Hymen <note>The Greek god of marriage ceremonies; the author is critiquing the pursuit of pleasure without the responsibilities of family.</note>. <meta>Continuity established with page 324; the catchword "Die" (The) from the previous page correctly introduces the first sentence here.</meta>

to be forced to see those who daily desecrate his altars.

We live in times of **Enlightenment** <note>German: *Aufklärung*; the 18th-century intellectual movement emphasizing reason over tradition.</note> and **refinement**, yet we lose more and more of our humanity; our senses become coarser and our desires ever more animalistic. The taste for decent pleasures, for the delicate feelings of innocent joys, is now a thing of the past; the oversatiated heart of today’s human no longer participates in the gentle joys of Nature.

When chance <note>German: *das Ohngefähr*; implying a lack of intentional moral purpose in modern unions.</note> gifts one of our fashionable married couples with an heir, is he not immediately regarded as an unwelcome witness to their love, whom one cannot send away soon enough? Shameful! — The mother has become a stumbling block <note>A biblical allusion to a cause of sin or moral failure.</note> to her own daughters, and the son blushes at the sight of his father, finding his example vexing. And the children do not wait long to become the worthy imitators of their fathers. But must it not be so, when man finds his account better served by **vice** than by **virtue**? Sordid gallantry <note>German: *Schmutzige Galanterie*; refers here to superficial, often adulterous, social etiquette.</note> leads to honors and dignities. **Luxury** is the murderer of **morals**, and domestic virtue is out of fashion.

--- Page 344 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the decline of social values or the historical shift in human behavior, leading into this critique of modern corruption. Catchword "Man" refers to the general "one" or "people" in German.</meta>

Time transforms everything. Formerly, commerce among men was an exchange of benefits; now, hospitality has become a shameful trade.

Where is the virtue of our fathers? Who still recognizes its dignity? Does it not seem to have lost its value, since prices are set upon it, as if it were not already reward enough in itself? — Is a good deed not paid for like any other purchasable commodity, and are the crowns of honor not offered for sale to the highest bidder?

The arts have fallen from their sublime dignity, and nothing great remains in them; poetry, the first and most beautiful of human talents, shamelessly offers itself for sale to every fool <note>original: "Gecken" - a dandy or shallow person obsessed with fashion and status</note> who wishes to buy its rhymes.

Flattery, vapid and ambiguous wit, and erroneous, audacious sophistry <note>original: "Vernünfteleyen" - a pejorative term for over-rationalizing or false reasoning</note> have become the content of most poems. Souls crawl in the dust, and few soar from the earth toward heaven on eagles' wings to praise the Almighty in lofty songs; the eloquence of great spirits has entered the service of lies and is no longer heard from the seat of truth.

--- Page 345 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrator's lamentation regarding the decline of religious devotion and the loss of human liberty, contrasting the present corruption with a lost golden age.</meta>

Temples are still built for you, but with negligent, idle hands; and the great palaces of pleasure overshadow the house of the Lord. His servants are ashamed of the garments they wear and change their clothes. The cloak lies heavy upon the shoulders of the <term>Cenobites</term><note>Members of a monastic community who live together, as opposed to hermits who live alone.</note>, which they are ashamed to wear.

God of my fathers! Why did you preserve me to see this abomination!! — Forgive the weak man who is pressed to the ground by the sight of these shameful scenes; why did you not let me come into the world in the golden age of the <term>Patriarchen</term><note>The biblical "fathers" of the faith, such as Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, often associated with a simpler, more virtuous era.</note>? — They have possessed the earth everywhere, and there is no longer any cave that could grant refuge to freedom. Alas! Shall I nevermore, before I descend into the clefts of death, be able to make use of the natural rights of man for even a single moment? Is the grave alone the only secure entrenchment against injustice and slavery?

God of wisdom! The life of men is short; it would not be well done to increase our days like the grains of sand on the shore of the sea. In your infinite wisdom, you surely foresaw what use we would make of them.

--- Page 346 ---
Those who let their days slip away unused are truly not so guilty; but those who apply them to evil—they justify all too well your wise economy <note>original: "Sparsamkeit"; here referring to the divine or natural thriftiness in granting time and life</note>. What kind of use do we actually make of our lives? Vain arts and other foolish occupations take up almost its entire span.

The rich alone enjoy the fruits of the spirit, and talent, debased by poverty, stays awake through the nights of its life for wealthy fools. But from all these glittering labors, what remains for us? A vain name and hardship. Oh, how happy is the wise man who consecrates his life only to the contemplation of nature, and rejoices in her works without mixing them with his own.

The God of nature is the inexhaustible source of true pleasures. To be a good and happy person, nothing more is required than that we return to nature and remain faithful to her.

God of my fathers! Now it is time; call those few beautiful days back to the earth—bring them among us again, those serene, happy days that man should never have forgotten, and whose memory was the only joy of poor Aglais <note>Aglais is likely a reference to a character from 18th-century pastoral or philosophical literature, symbolizing a simple, virtuous soul</note>.
